## 🧑 User  
*2026-07-18T17:29:02.350Z*

Good morning! I have a project I intend to take on and would like your help planning the path forward.

Over the last few months I have been experimenting with a handful of RGB LED matrix panels I bought a few years ago. The project evolved over time and eventually turned into `~/Projects/rayglow`. From the beginning to where I am now, I have learned *a lot* and enjoyed developing the software, firmware, and hardware. I recently finished assembling a new display consisting of 24x panels vs the original 8x and am running into signal integrity issues from chaining so many panels on a single data chain. Early in the project development I realized that using a FGPA would be an obvious solution over trying to bit-bang this much information with a rp2350b, so I looked around and eventually purchased a "ECP5 Evaluation Board". I continued to develop for the 8x chain set until it ran flawlessly on the 8x display, never utilizing the FGPA.

With the new setup I am at the fork in the road of contiuing by using multiple rp2350b's or switching over to the FGPA. This entire project has been about learning and in that spirit I want to work on developing an FPGA rendering path. I have known about FGPA chips for years but had never experimented with them. This is a great chance to do just that, so I want to give it a try.

I would like to use this folder as the dedicated project folder for the FGPA project. My eventual goal will be to use this project in conjunction with the RayGLow project to render shaders, but want to keep the projects separate. I am open to advice on how to approach this demarcation. This project will need to pull significantly from the RayGLow project but at the same time I do not want to interfere with the working Raspberry Pi 5 and rp2350b setup I currently have.

Here is an initial list of relevant context for the decisions moving forward:

- Review the RayGLow project `~/Projects/rayglow/` and rendering pipeline to fully understand the current setup.
- Review the FGPA hardware that I have, specifically the ECP5 Evaluation Board. `./reference/FPGA-EB-02017-1-3-ECP5-Evaluation-Board.pdf`
- Review the other documentation in `./reference`

From there, I'd like you to put together a proposed plan to integrate the FGPA as the translation layer for the 24x panel display.

I am open to any ideas, alternate approaches, thoughts, etc. 

One additional note is that there are some miscellaneous datasheets in the `./reference` folder that pertain to an ethernet adapter I was going to build for the ECP5 dev board. I have the parts but nothing is assembled. I'm still on the fence about this because if I use the Raspberry Pi 5, the dev board already has a built in header and it doesn't make sense to pass the information to the board through the RJ45 when it is already mated with the Pi.

8	The Pi5→RP2350 SPI link is the proven throughput wall: default 24 MHz (`render/__main__.py` `--spi-hz`), giving a ~46 FPS ceiling at 64 KB/frame on the 256×64 two-chain wall (`24e6/8/65536`). FPS scales inversely with byte count (≈55 FPS at 32 KB, ≈30 at 64 KB) — the signature of a transport-bound system. By contrast the RP2350 PIO BCM scan-out engine could do ~1000+ FPS; the *next* Pi-side wall after SPI is render-adjacent overhead (glReadPixels GPU→CPU stall + numpy `pack()` loop + submit), NOT the fragment shader itself.

# RayGlow-FPGA: Proposed Plan

## TL;DR

Your ECP5 Evaluation Board has a 40-pin Raspberry Pi header (JP8) that carries **all 28 Pi GPIOs into FPGA bank 3** — and GPIO0–27 is exactly the Pi 5's **DPI (parallel RGB) interface**. So the recommended architecture is: the Pi treats the wall as a **monitor**. It scans out 384×128 RGB888 over DPI via KMS; the FPGA captures it into a double-buffered framebuffer in block RAM (it fits — no external memory needed), and fans out BCM scan-out to **4 short chains of 6 panels** instead of today's 2 chains of 12. That kills the signal-integrity problem (chain depth) and the transport bottleneck (readback→pack→send disappears entirely) in one architecture, with a fully open toolchain (yosys → nextpnr → Trellis → openFPGALoader), verified working with this exact board. The Ethernet adapter: park it — reasoning below.

## What I reviewed

The rayglow repo end-to-end (renderer, firmware, hardware docs, ROADMAP §5, the 2026-06-18 FPGA design session), the ECP5-EVN user guide, the ECP5 family datasheet, the panel/misc datasheets, and the shared rayglow memory. Two findings frame everything:

1. **Your SI problem is chain depth, not the RP2350.** Wall v2 runs 2 chains × 12 panels serpentined (768×32 electrical strips). Only 4-deep chains were ever validated (37.5 MHz on v1); ROADMAP §5 flagged 12-deep as *the* open risk, and it bit. Any fix means more, shorter chains — and more chains is precisely what an FPGA is good at (an RP2350 tops out at 2 chains because `out pins, 16` and SRAM; the ECP5 has 178 user I/Os and true hardware parallelism).
2. **The framebuffer fits on-chip.** 384×128 × 24 bpp = 1.13 Mbit; double-buffered = 2.25 Mbit against the LFE5UM5G-85F's **3.74 Mbit of EBR**. No SDRAM, no DDR controller, no memory-arbitration complexity — the single biggest simplification available to a first FPGA project. Even a future 512×128 wall still double-buffers on-chip (3.0 Mbit); the RP2350's 512 KB SRAM ceiling stops mattering.

## Proposed architecture

```
will-desktop ──UDP :5005 (audio features, unchanged)──▶
Pi 5: rayglow renderer (GLSL/EGL, unchanged) ──▶ KMS DPI output, GPIO0–27
        RGB888 + PCLK/DE/HSYNC/VSYNC, custom modeline ~3.5 MHz @ 60 Hz
                        │  40-pin ribbon to JP8
ECP5-EVN: DPI capture (PCLK domain) ──▶ double-buffered EBR framebuffer
        swap on VSYNC (tear-free) ──▶ gamma LUT 8→11/12-bit ──▶ BCM scan-out engine
                        │  74AHCT245 ×8 (3.3→5 V)
4 chains × 6 panels (384×32 strips) — HUB75, 1/16 scan, shared A–D/LAT/OE/CLK
```

**Why DPI beats the alternatives:**

- **vs. reusing the 4-lane PIO protocol:** DPI deletes the Pi-side resolve-readback → `pack()` → transport chain *and* the whole custom wire protocol. The Pi's display controller does the scan-out in hardware; the renderer's only change is where the frame goes (a KMS plane instead of a dmabuf readback). Rendering and "sending" become the same operation. The 4-lane protocol remains a proven fallback if DPI surprises us.
- **vs. HDMI/TMDS RX (the "elegant endgame" from the June session):** same architectural result, but TMDS deserialization is a genuinely hard first FPGA project, and the EVN has no HDMI connector anyway. DPI is electrically just "synchronous parallel data + clock" — the gentlest possible ingest. HDMI stays a possible gateware v2.
- **vs. multiple RP2350s:** works, but no new learning, still SRAM-capped, and multiplies the custom-protocol plumbing.
- A practical bonus: DPI's ~3.5 MHz pixel clock is **within your PicoScope's 10 MHz bandwidth**. For once, the link you're bringing up is one your bench can actually see. (RP1 quirk to respect: DE must be output on GPIO1, which the rgb888 mode does anyway; there's a known color-order quirk on Pi 5 DPI worth checking at bring-up — trivially fixed by swizzling wires inside the FPGA if present.)

**Chain math** (why 4×6 fixes both SI and refresh): a 384-px strip shifts in 15.4 µs at 25 MHz. Shift-bound refresh ≈ 1/(16 rows × 8 planes × 15.4 µs) ≈ **500 Hz, vs ~143 Hz today** — and each chain is only 6 panels deep, comfortably near the validated-4 regime (and you can drop the clock with headroom to spare). If SI or refresh still disappoints, 8 chains × 3 panels doubles the margin again; the gateware should take chain count as a parameter from day one. I/O cost is trivial: 4×6 RGB + shared A–D/LAT/OE/CLK ≈ 31 pins against 90+ available on J32/J33/Versa headers (bank 7, 3.3 V). Level shifting: 8× '245 for four unshared-control chains — you have 10 SOIC + the VSSOP tape. Re-cabling the wall from 2×12 serpentine to 4×6 is just moving IDC jumpers and running two more ribbons back to the controller — and one chain per panel row means **the serpentine fold disappears entirely** (geometry becomes the identity).

**Quality upgrade for free:** today the Pi bakes gamma 2.1 into 8-bit values that feed 8-plane BCM — you lose shadow resolution twice. New contract: Pi sends perceptually-encoded 8-bit RGB; the FPGA expands through a 256→12-bit LUT into 10–12-plane BCM (the hzeller/CIE trick). Smoother low-brightness gradients than the wall has ever had, and EBR has room for it.

## Phases

**Phase 0 — Toolchain + board bring-up.** Install [OSS CAD Suite](https://github.com/YosysHQ/oss-cad-suite-build) (one tarball: yosys, nextpnr-ecp5, ecppack, openFPGALoader, iverilog/verilator, gtkwave/surfer); udev rule for the FTDI; blinky → buttons → UART → PLL from the 200 MHz X2 oscillator. Gotchas already found for you: the 12 MHz FTDI clock only exists while USB is plugged (JP2 installed) — treat X2 as the real system clock; and the FTDI↔ECP5 UART path is **not populated** (R34/R35 DNI) — use PMOD J31 + your Waveshare USB-UART bridge for a debug console instead. Concepts: LUTs/FFs/EBR, constraints (LPF), timing reports.

**Phase 1 — HUB75 core, one bench panel.** The heart of the project: shift/latch/address FSM + BCM OE engine, driving a **retired P6 panel** from an EBR test pattern — the live wall and RP2350 setup stay completely untouched. Simulation-first (this is where FPGA development actually happens — the culture matches your `verify.py` golden-frame instincts exactly: the testbench can check gateware output against the same numpy reference frames). First light can even be unbuffered 3.3 V at low clock — v1 ran 4 panels that way — then add '245s on the EVN's prototype area. Concepts: FSMs, dual-port RAM, timing closure.

**Phase 2 — DPI ingest.** Configure the Pi with `vc4-kms-dpi-generic` (rgb888, custom 384×128 modeline), capture in the PCLK domain, write into the double-buffer, swap on VSYNC. The magic milestone: **the wall renders the Pi's Linux console before rayglow is involved at all** — it's just a monitor. Then rayglow grows an additive `--output kms` mode (start by blitting the resolved frame into a DRM dumb buffer; graduate to direct GPU scan-out later). Concepts: clock-domain crossing — the flagship FPGA lesson.

**Phase 3 — Scale to the wall + wing PCB.** Re-cable to 4×6, validate one full 6-panel chain, then a KiCAD wing board (J32/J33 breakout → '245s → 4× IDC-16 + series termination + star-ground per POWER-AND-GROUNDING.md — same SKiDL/kicad-mcp flow as the HAT). Note from the inventory: 25–30 MHz chain SI validation is beyond the PicoScope, same as before — the sim-first workflow and conservative clocking mitigate, but the "used ≥100 MHz scope" gap from the manifest still stands if you want to *see* it.

**Phase 4 — Polish.** 10–12-plane BCM + brightness control, 120 Hz DPI if wanted, bitstream in SPI flash (MSPI boot via the CFGMDN switches → standalone power-on), and the EVN's mini-USB plugged into the Pi so you can reflash the FPGA over SSH from the desktop.

## The Ethernet adapter: park it

Your instinct is right, and the argument is crisper than "redundant": **the Pi has to stay regardless** — it's the renderer, and the whole audio-feature pipeline terminates there. Ethernet into the FPGA would only matter in a Pi-less architecture, which would mean reimplementing the GLES renderer in gateware — a different (enormous) project. Meanwhile the adapter itself is a hand-soldered KSZ9031 QFN doing RGMII at 125 MHz DDR, debugged blind on a 10 MHz scope — a brutal first FPGA project, for zero benefit over a header that's already there. Keep the parts (they're now catalogued in the lab manifest); if "wall without a Pi" ever becomes a real goal, the honest comparison at that point is a $20 Colorlight 5A-75B, which *is* an Ethernet HUB75 card — though note its ECP5-25F has only 1.0 Mbit of EBR, so it'd need its SDRAM where your 85F doesn't.

## Demarcation between the projects

- **`rayglow-fpga` (this repo):** gateware, testbenches, LPF constraints, build scripts, the wing-board KiCAD project, bring-up docs — and the single source of truth: an **INTERFACE-CONTRACT.md** defining the DPI modeline, pixel format, gamma ownership, and chain map. Move `reference/` → `.reference/` per your convention (vendor blobs, git-ignored).
- **`rayglow`:** gains one additive, flag-guarded `--output kms` mode and a link to the contract doc. Nothing else changes; the PIO/RP2350 path keeps working and keeps driving the wall throughout development (drop `DATA_CLK_DIV` if you need it stable in the meantime).
- Both cwds already share the same memory pool, so context flows between sessions automatically.

## Decisions I'd like from you

1. **HDL: Amaranth (my recommendation) or SystemVerilog?** Amaranth is a Python-embedded HDL from the open-toolchain world — `uv add amaranth`, elaborates through yosys natively, simulator is pure Python (testbenches could literally import rayglow's packer for golden checks), and it removes Verilog's classic footguns (blocking/non-blocking, accidental latches) while keeping every concept visible — you can dump the generated Verilog/RTLIL anytime. The honest counterargument: the ecosystem's tutorials and cores (Project F, hzeller-adjacent HUB75 work) are Verilog, so you'd be translating as you learn — though translating is itself good learning, and reading Verilog is on the path either way. SystemVerilog + cocotb (Python testbenches) is the fully-standard alternative; the phases are identical either way.
2. **Chain topology:** 4×6 (my default) vs 8×3 (double margin, double ribbons) — affects the wing board, so worth settling by end of Phase 1.
3. **Ethernet:** confirm parking it.

If the direction looks right, my next step is scaffolding this folder with the `new-project` skill (hardware shape) and writing the plan + interface contract in as founding docs.

Sources: [Pi 5 DPI overlay & RGB888 modes](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/raspberrypi/firmware/master/boot/overlays/README), [Using a DPI Display whitepaper](https://pip-assets.raspberrypi.com/categories/685-whitepapers-app-notes/documents/RP-003471-WP/Using-a-DPI-display), [RP1 DPI on Pi 5 (interlace/DE notes)](https://www.raspberrypi.com/news/how-we-added-interlaced-video-to-raspberry-pi-5/), [Pi 5 DPI color-order issue](https://github.com/raspberrypi/linux/issues/6505), [open toolchain on the ECP5-EVN](https://www.jjhorton.co.uk/posts/ecp5g-eval/)

# new-project

1. Establish (ask only what's not obvious from the request): name (kebab-case), shape
   (**hardware** | **tool**), one-line purpose, primary language.
2. `mkdir ~/Projects/<name>`, copy the matching `templates/` tree from this skill's directory,
   then fill `{{NAME}}`, `{{PURPOSE}}`, `{{DATE}}` (today, YYYY-MM-DD) in the copies.
3. **hardware:** copy `templates/hardware/` → README.md, CLAUDE.md, will-notes.md,
   `gitignore` → `.gitignore`. Also `mkdir -p .reference docs/design-history hardware tools`.
   Always `git init` + an initial commit.
4. **tool:** copy `templates/tool/README.md` only. Ask whether git is wanted (docbatch
   precedent: optional for small tools).
5. Language init: Python → `uv init` (never pip/venv); Rust → `cargo init`; C++/embedded →
   skip, structure is manual.
6. Do not create empty placeholder docs beyond the templates — files earn their existence.
7. Remind Will: datasheets go in `.reference/<component>/` (one folder per chip/board —
   PDF + vendor HTML + pinout images), which is git-ignored but agent-readable.


ARGUMENTS: rayglow-fpga — hardware/embedded shape, folder already exists at ~/Projects/rayglow-fpga with a reference/ dir to convert to .reference/. FPGA gateware project (Amaranth HDL, ECP5-EVN) acting as the HUB75 translation layer for the rayglow LED wall.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-18T18:16:55.804Z*

Project scaffolded, committed, and the first simulation is green. Here's where things stand:

## What's in place

**Repo structure** (2 commits on `main`): founding docs — `README.md` (orientation + decision log), `CLAUDE.md` (agent guidance + verified board facts), `ROADMAP.md` (Phases 0–4 with acceptance criteria and the governing numbers), `INTERFACE-CONTRACT.md` (the versioned rayglow ↔ rayglow-fpga boundary: modeline, pixel format, gamma ownership, chain geometry), and `hardware/WING-BOARD.md`. Your `reference/` moved to git-ignored `.reference/` with one folder per component (`ecp5-evn/`, `ecp5/`, `rpi5/`, the P6 panel, and `ethernet-adapter/` for the parked stash) — future datasheets follow that pattern.

**Gateware skeleton, already runnable**: a uv project with Amaranth 0.5.9. This is the nice thing about the Amaranth choice — simulation needs *nothing* beyond uv (it bundles a yosys wheel), so `uv run pytest` passes today: a `Blinky` component, the ECP5-EVN platform file (only pins I verified against the user guide: 12 MHz clock, 8 LEDs, button), and a testbench asserting the LED tracks the counter MSB cycle-for-cycle. The bitstream build (`uv run python -m gateware.top_blinky`) is written but waits on Phase 0's toolchain.

## Your modular board question — one real implication found

The idea is sound, and it survives contact with the pin budget only because of one trick, now codified in `WING-BOARD.md`: J32+J33 give exactly 32 GPIOs, but two boards × 19 signals = 38. The fix is a fast/slow split — each board gets its own 12 RGB + CLK (13 pins, point-to-point), while A–D/LAT/OE (6 pins) are **Y-bused to all boards** via the harness. Those six only change at row rate, so two '245 loads on a 3.3 V line cost nothing, and 2×13+6 = 32 fits J32/J33 exactly; the 8-chain future (58 pins) fits with the Versa headers. The other two rules that keep the PCB universal: a board owns *complete* chains (board-to-board skew can never matter), and boards attach by crimped ribbon harness rather than stacking — the harness absorbs every header-pinout difference, including the Versa headers' different layout, so the PCB never changes.

## Phase 0, whenever you're ready

Toolchain install is the one step that touches your system, so I left it for your go-ahead:

```fish
# OSS CAD Suite nightly (yosys + nextpnr-ecp5 + ecppack + openFPGALoader + gtkwave, one tarball)
mkdir -p ~/opt; and curl -L <latest release tarball> | tar xz -C ~/opt
fish_add_path ~/opt/oss-cad-suite/bin
```

plus a udev rule for the FT2232H. Say the word and I'll do the install, wire up the udev rule, and we can put the first bitstream on the board — LED0 blinking at 1.4 Hz is Phase 0's finish line.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-18T19:00:26.203Z*

Toolchain is in and the first bitstream is built. Summary:

**Done:**
- **OSS CAD Suite** (today's nightly, conveniently) extracted to `~/opt/oss-cad-suite`, appended to your fish PATH via `fish_add_path --append` — appended rather than prepended so its bundled tools never shadow system ones (python lives in a separate `py3bin/` anyway). Verified: yosys 0.67, nextpnr-ecp5 0.10, ecppack 1.4, openFPGALoader 1.1.1.
- **First bitstream**: `uv run python -m gateware.top_blinky` ran the whole pipeline — Amaranth → RTLIL → yosys synth → nextpnr place-and-route → ecppack → `build/top.bit`. The design uses 25 flip-flops and 32 LUTs of 83,640 (a rounding error of the chip), and nextpnr reports **Fmax 589 MHz against the 12 MHz constraint** — timing reports will get a lot more interesting once the BCM engine exists.
- Two things in `build/` worth a look for the learning angle: `top.debug.v` is the Verilog Amaranth generated (your bridge to reading ecosystem code — compare it against the ~15 lines of `gateware/blinky.py`), and `top.lpf` is the constraint file nextpnr consumed — that's where the platform file's pin/IO_TYPE declarations end up.

**Two steps need you:**

1. The udev rule needs sudo — run this (the `!` prefix executes it in this session):
```
! echo 'SUBSYSTEM=="usb", ATTRS{idVendor}=="0403", ATTRS{idProduct}=="6010", TAG+="uaccess"' | sudo tee /etc/udev/rules.d/99-ecp5-evn.rules; and sudo udevadm control --reload; and sudo udevadm trigger
```
`TAG+="uaccess"` is the modern Arch way — systemd-logind grants the device to whoever is physically seated, no `plugdev` group needed. `0403:6010` is the FT2232H's VID:PID.

2. Plug in the board (mini-USB, and check **JP2 is installed / JP1 removed** so the 12 MHz FTDI clock reaches the fabric — per UG Table 4.1 that clock only exists via USB). Then I'll run `openFPGALoader --detect` and load `build/top.bit` — LED0 blinking at 1.4 Hz closes out the first half of Phase 0.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-18T23:18:06.028Z*

Bitstream loaded — SRAM erase, load, and configuration enable all completed cleanly. The board can't lie to me from here though, so the eyes-on check is yours:

- The green **DONE LED (D4)** should be lit — that's the ECP5 confirming it accepted the configuration (UG Table 7.5).
- One of the eight red user LEDs (**D5–D12** cluster, top-right near the DIP switches) should be blinking at ~1.4 Hz — that's `counter[22]` rolling over at 12 MHz.

If it's blinking: that's first light on your first FPGA — the full open pipeline (Amaranth → yosys → nextpnr → ecppack → openFPGALoader) is proven end-to-end, and this loads survive-until-power-off into SRAM in about two seconds, which is the dev loop we'll live in from now on.

Worth 60 seconds while you're looking at the board: `bat ~/Projects/rayglow-fpga/build/top.debug.v` — that's the Verilog equivalent of the 15-line `blinky.py`, and the `TRELLIS_FF`/LUT soup below it in `top.json` is what nextpnr actually placed. The `counter[22]` you see in the Verilog is physically 23 flip-flops in one slice column right now.

Confirm the blink and I'll check off the milestone; next up in Phase 0 is button→LED (30 minutes of fun), then the UART console via PMOD J31 + your Waveshare bridge, then the PLL from the 200 MHz oscillator — the piece the DPI capture will eventually hang off.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-18T23:34:13.336Z*

A new design is on the board right now — go play with it:

- **LED D12** (LED7) is the heartbeat at 1.4 Hz
- **Hold SW4** (the general-purpose push button) → **D5** lights while held
- **Flip DIP switches 1–6 on SW5** → LEDs D6–D11 track them live

All of it is pure combinational wiring plus the counter — `gateware/top_phase0.py` is 20 lines. Note what *didn't* happen: no debouncing, no polling loop, no interrupt handler. The switch literally becomes a wire to the LED through the fabric. That's the mental shift FPGA work keeps delivering.

I also pulled the schematic appendix (Figs A.5–A.12) while extending the platform file, which paid off twice: JP8's GPIOs run **straight into bank 3 with no series resistors** and VCCIO3 is hard-strapped to 3.3 V — both exactly what the DPI capture wants — and I caught a discrepancy where Table 2.1 claims VCCIO1 defaults to 2.5 V but the schematic shows the 3.3 V strap populated (noted in `platform.py`; harmless either way).

## Your sidebar: FPGA as an audio DSP

Short answer: **yes, it's viable, both roles fit on this one chip simultaneously, and you don't need more hardware.** Now recorded as Phase 5 in the ROADMAP. The reasoning:

**Resource math.** The 85F has 156 hardware 18×18 multipliers (sysDSP slices) that the HUB75 translator will use *zero* of — the translator is counters, comparators, and block RAM. An FFT is the canonical FPGA-DSP workload, and audio is glacially slow by fabric standards: a single time-multiplexed butterfly at 100 MHz finishes a 1024-point FFT in ~50 µs, while a new window of samples only arrives every ~20 ms at 48 kHz. You could compute the FFT 400× over between frames using one multiplier out of 156. Coexistence isn't the question; the FFT engine will rattle around in the empty space.

**Mic input is trivially easy.** An I2S MEMS mic (the INMP441-class parts from your esp32-mic work) needs 3 GPIOs — the FPGA generates BCLK/WS and shifts in SD. That's a Phase-1-difficulty module. PDM mics work too and are their own classic lesson (CIC decimation).

**The real design question is architectural, not computational:** who consumes the features? The Pi renderer does — they arrive as shader uniforms/textures. So an FPGA FFT doesn't slot in as "processing on the way to the display"; it has to send features *up* to the Pi. And here's where it gets elegant: rayglow already treats senders as swappable producers of the feed-v3 packet (desktop pipewire sender, fake_sender, your ESP32-S3 offshoot). The FPGA becomes **one more sender** — mic → window/FFT/band-fold in fabric → feed-v3 packets over the FT2232H's second channel, up the same EVN-USB-to-Pi cable we already planned for reflashing. Same contract, new producer, zero renderer changes.

**Honest assessment:** as a *system* improvement it's marginal — numpy FFT on the desktop is effectively free and the sender's envelope/beat work is already good. But as a *learning* project it's the canonical second act: fixed-point arithmetic, windowing, DSP-slice inference, magnitude via CORDIC, ping-pong buffering — and every prerequisite skill is exactly what Phases 1–2 teach (EBR, CDC, sim-first verification). Do it after the wall works. The bonus that makes it worth it: with mic, FFT, and scan-out all on one chip, the wall can render spectrum bars **with no Pi attached at all** — a genuinely useful bring-up diagnostic and a properly demoscene party trick.

**Remaining in Phase 0** whenever you're ready: the PLL milestone (multiply 12 MHz → 60 MHz with the EHXPLLL primitive — your first taste of clock resources and the foundation for the scan-out clock domain), and the debug UART once you wire the Waveshare bridge to PMOD J31 (pins 1/2 = balls C6/C7, 3.3 V bank 0). After that, Phase 1: the HUB75 core against a retired P6 panel.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-19T02:15:09.675Z*

The PLL design is loaded — here's what the board should be showing:

- **D12** (LED7): heartbeat at ~1.4 Hz — the 12 MHz `sync` domain
- **D5** (LED0): the *same 15 lines of Blinky code* at ~7.2 Hz — exactly 5× faster, because it now lives in the 60 MHz PLL domain
- **D6** (LED1): solid on — the PLL's LOCK output

The concepts this milestone banked, since they're load-bearing for everything ahead:

**The PLL math.** An ECP5 PLL can't synthesize 60 MHz directly — it runs an internal VCO that must land in 400–800 MHz, and everything is integer dividers around it: 12 MHz × CLKFB_DIV(5) × CLKOP_DIV(10) = 600 MHz VCO, ÷10 back out = 60 MHz. `ecppll -i 12 -o 60` did that solve; `gateware/pll.py` is its output translated into an Amaranth `Instance` with the why documented. Not every frequency is reachable from every input — a constraint that'll matter when we pick the HUB75 shift clock.

**Lock-gated reset.** Between power-up and LOCK, a PLL's output is garbage — edges at wrong frequencies as the VCO hunts. The wrapper holds the `fast` domain in reset until LOCK asserts (`ResetSignal("fast").eq(~locked)`), so no flip-flop in that domain ever clocks on a junk edge. Skipping this is a classic source of "works most boots" bugs.

**`DomainRenamer` is the payoff of Amaranth's explicit-domain model.** `Blinky` was written once against the abstract `sync` domain; `DomainRenamer("fast")(Blinky(...))` re-homes it without touching its code. The DPI capture (PCLK domain) and the HUB75 scan-out (PLL domain) will be built exactly this way, and the multi-clock simulation in `tests/test_domains.py` — two clocks advancing at 12 and 60 MHz in one sim — is the exact harness pattern we'll use to test the clock-domain crossing between them. (The EHXPLLL itself can't be simulated — it's an analog block — so the sim drives the domain with its own clock, which is precisely what the PLL does on hardware.)

**The timing report grew a second entry**: `fast_clk` now passes at 60 MHz with 491 MHz of headroom. Trivial today, but this is the report that turns into a negotiation once the scan-out engine has real logic depth in that domain.

That closes Phase 0 except for the debug UART, which needs hardware from you: wire the **Waveshare bridge to PMOD J31** — pin 1 (ball C6) = FPGA TX → bridge RX, pin 2 (C7) = FPGA RX ← bridge TX, pin 5 = GND (pins 6/12 are VCCIO0 3.3 V — don't feed them from the bridge, just share ground). Once that's connected I'll write the UART core — first real FSM of the project, with a sim against a golden byte stream — and after that we're into Phase 1: the HUB75 engine and first light on a P6 panel.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-19T03:19:40.920Z*

Yes — that works directly, no Waveshare needed. The `debugprobe` firmware (2e8a:000c) is two devices in one: the CMSIS-DAP probe you've been using, **plus a USB-CDC UART bridge** that shows up as `/dev/ttyACM0`. On a bare Pico running debugprobe, the UART side is GP4 = probe TX, GP5 = probe RX, and the baud rate is whatever the host terminal sets — it's passed straight through to the hardware UART. 3.3 V logic, same as the FPGA's bank 0. Conveniently, the three pins you need are physically adjacent on the Pico: **pins 6, 7, 8 = GP4, GP5, GND**.

Wiring (three jumpers):

| Pico (debugprobe) | ECP5-EVN J31 (PMOD) | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| GP4 / pin 6 (probe TX) | J31 pin 2 (ball C7) | → FPGA RX |
| GP5 / pin 7 (probe RX) | J31 pin 1 (ball C6) | ← FPGA TX |
| GND / pin 8 | J31 pin 5 | common ground |

(Don't connect the Pico's 3V3 to J31 pins 6/12 — those are the EVN's own VCCIO0 rail; ground is the only thing to share.)

Meanwhile I'll build the UART gateware — the project's first real FSM — so it's on the board by the time you've run the jumpers:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-19T03:21:14.503Z*

The UART design is on the board and waiting for wires. Two things worth noticing from this build:

- **Fmax dropped from 589 → 348 MHz.** Nothing is wrong — the design finally has real logic depth (the RX FSM's timer compare feeding state decisions). This is the number to watch as designs grow; at 12 MHz we have a 29× margin, but the HUB75 engine at 25–30 MHz with wide datapaths is where it starts being a conversation.
- The two FSM subtleties the sims pin down, because they're *the* classic UART bugs: the RX samples **mid-bit** (waits 1.5 bit-periods after the start edge, then every 1.0 — sampling on edges instead is the canonical off-by-half error), and the TX holds the stop bit for a **full bit period** before re-asserting `ready`, which is what makes back-to-back bytes legal — the round-trip test sends five bytes with zero gap to prove it.

Once your three jumpers are in (Pico pin 6/GP4 → J31 pin 2, Pico pin 7/GP5 → J31 pin 1, Pico pin 8/GND → J31 pin 5), tell me and I'll drive the test from here — open the debugprobe's `/dev/ttyACM*` at 115200, watch for the `.` beacon (TX proof), then fire bytes at it and check the echo (RX proof). You'll also see whatever you send rendered in binary on the eight LEDs — type `U` (0x55) for the full alternating pattern.

*2026-07-19T05:12:47.469Z*

The diagnostic is loaded and I've streamed 3,200 bytes of `0x55` out of the probe's TX wire. Every J31 data pin is now a pulled-up input with a latch — exactly one LED should have lit and stayed lit, telling us where that wire physically landed:

| Lit LED | Means probe-TX is on | Diagnosis |
|---|---|---|
| **D6** (LED1) | J31 pin 2 (C7) | Wiring is correct — the fault is elsewhere (I'll dig on my side) |
| **D5** (LED0) | J31 pin 1 (C6) | TX/RX swapped — move each signal wire to the other J31 pin |
| **D7/D8** | pin 3 or 4 | Off-by-one along the row |
| **D9/D10** | pin 7 or 8 (C8/B8) | Right position, wrong row of the 2×6 header |
| **none** | — | No contact at all — check the jumper seating and that GND is on pin 5 |

Which LED is lit?

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-19T05:17:40.154Z*

D6 alone — so the probe's TX wire is sitting exactly where it belongs (J31 pin 2 → C7), bytes are physically arriving at the FPGA, and the header orientation is right. That exonerates half the link. Note that D5 staying dark proves nothing about the other wire: the probe's RX pin never drives edges, so the diag can't see it.

Next bisection, fully electronic: a bitstream that's just a **wire** — C7 input directly to C6 output, no UART logic at all. If bytes echo through that, both jumpers and the probe are good and the fault is in my UART glue; if not, it's the C6 jumper or the probe's RX side.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-19T05:21:17.908Z*

**Phase 0 is officially closed** — everything committed, memory and docs updated. In two sessions you went from "never touched an FPGA" to: a working open-source toolchain, five bitstreams built and loaded from the CLI, a second clock domain from a PLL you can explain, a UART you wrote as your first FSMs (verified byte-perfect over real wire), and a debugged hardware mystery with a proper bisection. The one unchecked Phase 0 box is yours when you feel like it: skim the sysMEM/EBR and sysCLOCK PLL sections of the family datasheet in `.reference/ecp5/` — both blocks are about to become main characters.

**Next up is Phase 1, the heart of the project**: the HUB75 BCM scan-out engine. The plan from the ROADMAP:

1. **Sim first, panel later.** The engine (row FSM + pixel shifter + binary-coded-modulation OE scheduler, parametric in chains/panels/bit-depth) gets built and verified entirely in pytest before any wire moves — including a Python golden model that reconstructs the displayed image from the simulated pin wiggles, `verify.py`-style. This is the part where the FPGA culture and your rayglow culture turn out to be the same culture.
2. **Then first light** on a retired P6 panel — direct 3.3 V drive at a gentle clock, short ribbon, exactly like wall v1 ran. For that session you'll want on the bench: one P6 panel, a 5 V supply for it, an IDC-16 ribbon, and a handful of jumpers from J32 (I'll give you the exact pin map once the engine's pins are frozen). The live wall and its RP2350 driver stay untouched throughout.

Say the word and I'll start on the engine — design notes first, then the modules and sims.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-19T05:46:49.522Z*

**The scan-out engine is written, verified, and built** — the core of the whole project now exists and passes its golden-model tests. What landed:

**`gateware/scanout.py`** — `Hub75Core`, parametric in chains/width/scan/planes/unit. Architecture per the plan: one dual-port EBR bank per chain-half holding raw RGB888 (identical to the future DPI wire format — the ingest will simply write the other port), CIE1931 gamma LUT applied at readout, single bit-select per BCM plane, and a four-state FSM (`PRELOAD → SHIFT → LATCH → DISPLAY`) with a 2-stage read pipeline that exactly matches one pixel slot. Deliberately sequential v1 — the shift/display overlap that doubles refresh is a recorded v2 in the doc.

**The golden-model test** is the part I'd read first (`tests/test_scanout.py`): the testbench *is a software panel* — it clocks RGB bits into a model shift register on real CLK edges, latches on LAT, and integrates every pixel's lit-cycles while OE is active. Over a full frame, BCM's defining property must hold exactly: **lit-time = U × lut[source]**, for every pixel, every channel, both chain configurations. It passed on the first run, which is the sim-first workflow paying out — the FSM, the pipeline alignment, and the bit packing were all debugged before any hardware existed.

**`top_hub75.py` bitstream is built** (4 block RAMs, Fmax 172 MHz, running in the 12 MHz domain → 6 MHz shift clock, ~139 Hz refresh at 76% duty — all derived in `gateware/SCANOUT.md`).

**Your part — the bench setup for first light.** From `SCANOUT.md` (full table there):

1. A retired **P6 panel**, its **input** connector (the panels are directional — data flows in one side, out the other).
2. **13 signal jumpers** from J32 to the HUB75 header — the signals sit on the first 13 GPIO positions of J32 in table order (pins 5, 6, 9, 10, 13, 14 → R1 G1 B1 R2 G2 B2; pins 17, 18, 21, 22 → A B C D; pins 25, 26, 29 → CLK LAT OE).
3. **At least three ground returns** (HUB75 pins 4, 8, 16 to J32's abundant GND pins), one routed beside CLK — the "missing shared ground = complete noise" lesson from the PIO bus applies verbatim.
4. **Panel 5 V from a bench PSU** (the 150 W is plenty), never from the EVN; bond PSU(−) into the same ground star.

When it's wired: load `build/top.bit` (remember — power cycle means reload) and you should see the gradient with white/red/green/blue orientation corners. Any flip or rotation the panel shows us is diagnostic data, not failure — that's exactly what the corners are for. And your PicoScope can finally watch a whole HUB75 conversation: LAT at ~22 kHz and the binary-weighted OE intervals are both well inside its bandwidth — genuinely worth probing OE while it runs; you'll *see* the BCM weights.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-20T09:58:49.634Z*

Stop — don't recheck the HUB75 wiring at all yet. The problem is upstream of everything you soldered. **The board isn't enumerating on USB.** `openFPGALoader --detect` gets "device not found," and `lsusb` confirms it: your Pico is there (2e8a:000c), but the EVN's FT2232H (0403:6010) has completely vanished — and so have its two `/dev/ttyUSB*` interfaces that were present on the 18th. 

That matters because the FTDI chip is *how the computer reaches the FPGA to configure it at all*. If it's gone, the FPGA is unconfigured no matter how perfect your panel wiring is — nothing could display. Your jumpers and soldered pins are not the suspect right now.

Here's the key fact about this board's power tree, from the schematics (Fig A.3 + A.12): the FT2232H runs off the board's **+3.3 V rail, which is generated from the 12 V barrel jack — not from USB**. So the FTDI only enumerates when *both* the 12 V adapter and the mini-USB are connected. A "power cycle" that left the 12 V off, or knocked either connector loose, produces exactly this symptom. (That's also why blinky needed both all along — the 12 MHz clock comes from that same FTDI chip.)

Check these in order, using the board's own power LEDs (they'll teach you its state at a glance):

1. **12 V present?** Look at the cluster of indicator LEDs near the power section: **D26 is blue, labeled "12VIN GOOD"** and **D25 is green, "+3.3 V"** (also D31 +2.5 V, D24 +1.2 V core, D23 +1.5 V). 
   - **All dark** → the 12 V barrel jack (J37) lost power. Replug the wall adapter. This is my top suspect.
   - **Blue D26 lit but the green rail LEDs dark** → the 12 V is fine but a rail is being dragged down — most likely a *short in your new J32 wiring* (a signal pin touching ground, or two pins bridged). In that case: unplug all the HUB75 jumpers from J32 and see if the green LEDs come back. If they do, you've found it, and we test the panel wiring separately with a meter before reconnecting.

2. **Mini-USB seated?** J2 is the small mini-USB near the JTAG header — separate from the 12 V barrel. It's easy to nudge loose while working around the board. Reseat it firmly. If you can, try a different mini-USB cable — a surprising number are charge-only or intermittent.

The moment `lsusb` shows `0403:6010` again, say the word (or just tell me what the LEDs are doing) and I'll re-detect, reload the bitstream, and we're back to first-light. If it turns out to be the short in step 1, that's actually a lucky catch — far better to find it now with the rails than to have it quietly corrupting signals later.

That's a win — and a cleaner one than your description suggests. Let me decode what you're seeing against what the pattern encodes, because three of the four corners are textbook-correct and that tells us a lot.

The gradient is: **R = left→right, G = top→bottom, B = right→left.** So the four gradient corners *should* be:

| Corner | Expected | You saw | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| Top-left (R=0,G=0,B=255) | blue | purple-ish | ⚠ extra red |
| Top-right (R=255,G=0,B=0) | red | red | ✓ |
| Bottom-left (R=0,G=255,B=255) | cyan | blue-green | ✓ (cyan *is* blue-green) |
| Bottom-right (R=255,G=255,B=0) | yellow | yellow | ✓ |

And the single override pixels: bottom-right blue-on-yellow **pops** (you saw it), top-right red-on-red **blends** (as predicted), bottom-left green-on-cyan blends because green and cyan differ only in the blue channel — all expected. **Orientation is perfect** (no flip, no rotation), the shift is aligned (that lone blue pixel landing exactly in the bottom-right corner proves the right edge is pixel-accurate), and the ramps are smooth. Your gateware is doing exactly what the sim said it would. **First light achieved.** Let me mark it:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-20T10:13:38.822Z*

Now, the two blemishes — the purple top-left and the jitter. Both are analog, not logic (the sim integrates every pixel's exact on-time and it's correct), and both are the expected price of driving HUB75 straight off the FPGA's 3.3 V pins through loose jumpers with no buffering and no termination. Two distinct causes:

**The jitter** is signal integrity — bare jumper wires are unterminated transmission lines, the FPGA's I/O drive is weak into the panel's input capacitance, and adjacent wires crosstalk. This is exactly what the `74AHCT245` buffers fix: they restore clean, fast 5 V-tolerant edges with real drive behind them, which is why every commercial HUB75 setup buffers the lines. It's the planned next step for precisely this reason.

**The purple top-left** is more interesting and probably a *different* cause: the top-left is the darkest region of the whole image (R=0, G=0, only blue), and dark regions live in the low-order BCM planes. With `unit=4`, the least-significant plane is displayed for only 4 clocks — **333 ns** of OE-enable. The panel's constant-current drivers (ICN2012) may not switch cleanly on a pulse that short, so the darkest bits render imprecisely and pick up a color cast. A little red crosstalk on the unbuffered lines then tips blue → purple, and washes out that single white corner pixel. Both suspects point the same way.

Here's the honest part: I don't want to *guess* between them. Three ways forward, and this is your bench so you pick the order:

1. **Add the `74AHCT245` buffers** (the planned step). Biggest single lever — kills the jitter, likely reduces the tint too. You've got the SOIC parts and the EVN has a through-hole prototype area right there. This is the "real" answer.
2. **Scope it first** (the engineer's move, and your PicoScope can finally see everything here). Probe OE and CLK while it runs — LAT ticks at ~2.2 kHz, the BCM OE pulses are 333 ns to 340 µs, all comfortably inside 10 MHz. You'd *see* whether the LSB OE pulse is being mangled, confirming or killing the short-pulse theory before soldering anything.
3. **Cheap gateware experiment I can do right now**: rebuild with `unit` bumped (say 16) — that stretches the LSB pulse to ~1.3 µs. If the top-left tint cleans up, it was short-pulse nonlinearity; if only buffers fix the jitter, it was SI. Costs refresh (~139 → ~35 Hz, may flicker) but it's purely diagnostic and takes me one minute. I can also add solid-color and single-channel-ramp test patterns that isolate crosstalk directly.

My recommendation: let me do #3 right now (one-minute diagnostic build to classify the tint), you take a quick look, and *then* we commit to the buffers with a known cause rather than hoping. Want me to build the `unit`-bumped diagnostic and a couple of isolating patterns?

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-20T10:22:46.727Z*

The board is now showing **8 vertical color bars** — left to right: red, green, blue, cyan, magenta, yellow, white, black — each one bright at the top and fading to dark at the bottom. **SW5 switch position 1** is the live A/B knob (LED D12 lights when it's ON to confirm which mode you're in):

- **OFF → unit = 4** (production setting, ~139 Hz, rock steady)
- **ON → unit = 32** (OE pulses 8× longer, ~22 Hz — it *will* visibly flicker; that's the low refresh rate, not a fault)

Two things to read off it, and together they diagnose both suspects:

**1. The short-pulse theory (flip the switch back and forth):** Watch the **fading bottoms** of the bars, and especially the **blue bar**. If the colors are truer and the tint disappears when you switch **ON** (long pulses) versus OFF (short pulses), then the culprit is the panel's constant-current drivers not resolving the 333 ns least-significant-bit pulse cleanly — a gateware/timing matter, not your wiring. If the bottoms look about the same either way, short pulses aren't the problem.

**2. The crosstalk theory (look at the steady OFF mode):** Check the **blue bar** in isolation — is it clean blue, or purple/magenta? And check the **black bar** on the far right — is it truly dark, or does it glow? Pure blue turning purple, or black glowing, means energy from the neighboring saturated bars (or the bright red end) is coupling into lines that should be quiet — that's signal integrity on the bare jumpers, and the fix is buffering: your Adafruit HAT or the '245s.

The elegant part is these two failure modes point to *opposite* fixes, and this one pattern separates them. Tell me what you see in each case:
- Does the tint/color improve when you flip the switch ON?
- In steady OFF mode, is the blue bar clean, and is the black bar dark?

Agreed — move on. And the headline finding is the real result here: you didn't have a color bug, you had **flaky OE and GND solder joints**, which is exactly why touching the wires snapped it into place. That also retroactively explains the earlier gradient tint — a marginal OE line (OE is the master brightness/blanking control for the *entire* panel) plus a marginal ground return will corrupt levels and colors globally, which means my "short-pulse nonlinearity" theory was almost certainly chasing a symptom of the same bad joints. Good instinct trusting the physical layer.

The residual end-of-line skew on rows 14–19 (17 worst) is the classic signature of OE/clock edge integrity on the last-shifted pixels of a line — the pixels furthest along the shift register are the most sensitive to any timing or return-path weakness, so a still-marginal OE joint or the unbuffered lines would produce exactly a position-dependent skew. Not worth gateware effort now: the fix is the physical layer you're already planning (reseat those joints, then drop on the Adafruit HAT for buffered 5 V), and if any skew survives *that*, the gateware fallback is a blanking-guard interval — already recorded as a v2 item in `SCANOUT.md`. ## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-20T10:47:59.579Z*

Phase 1 is behind us with the engine proven, and **Phase 2 is underway**. Here's where things stand and what's next.

**Done this session:** the artifact turned out to be flaky OE/GND solder joints (recorded and parked as a physical-layer track — reseat + Adafruit HAT — that runs parallel to gateware work), and I built the first piece of the DPI ingest: `DpiIn`, which converts the Pi's `DE`/`VSYNC` stream into pixel coordinates in the pixel-clock domain. It's modeline-agnostic (derives x/y from the data-enable edges rather than hardcoded porch numbers, so it tolerates whatever exact clock the RP1 lands on) and sim-verified over two frames — and the sim earned its keep by catching a polarity bug in the first-frame VSYNC detection that would only have shown up on hardware as a missing first frame.

**The next gateware step is the heart of Phase 2: clock-domain crossing.** The DPI decoder writes pixels in the *pixel-clock* domain; the scan-out engine reads them in its *own* domain. Bridging those two safely is the flagship FPGA lesson, and the mechanism is specific: a double-buffered framebuffer where the DPI side writes the back buffer, the scan-out side reads the front buffer, and the buffer-swap request (fired on `frame_start`) crosses the domain boundary through a 2-flop synchronizer. I already built `Hub75Core`'s framebuffer as dual-port EBR precisely so the second port is waiting for this. That's a fully sim-verifiable step, no hardware needed — I can carry it forward next.

**Two things that will need you when we get there** (not now): once the CDC framebuffer is built, Phase 2's payoff milestone is *the panel showing the Pi's Linux console with zero rayglow code involved* — pure "wall is a monitor." That requires (a) editing the Pi's `config.txt` with the `vc4-kms-dpi-generic` modeline (drafted in `INTERFACE-CONTRACT.md`), and (b) a 40-pin ribbon from the Pi's header to the EVN's JP8. And ideally the panel is buffered by then (Adafruit HAT) so we're validating video against a clean display rather than through marginal joints.

Want me to keep going and build the double-buffered CDC framebuffer next, or pause here so you can tackle the solder joints and buffering first?

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-20T11:02:51.294Z*

The capstone caught a real semantic bug — and a subtle one worth understanding. `DpiIn.frame_start` fires on the *first pixel* of a frame, but I was flipping the capture buffer at that same instant, which split each frame across both buffers (first pixel to the old buffer, the rest to the new one) and handed the reader an empty buffer. The fix: on `frame_start`, route *this* frame's very first pixel to the new buffer too, using a combinational "current capture buffer" that already reflects the flip:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-20T11:13:39.911Z*

Big progress while you're on the Pi/HAT work. Here's where Phase 2 stands.

**The translation layer is complete and verified in simulation.** The full path — Pi DPI video in → clock-domain crossing → HUB75 scan-out — now exists as gateware and passes an end-to-end test where a video frame injected on the pixel clock comes out byte-exact (after gamma) on the panel pins driven by a separate, faster scan clock. That's the conceptual heart of the whole project, done. 13/13 sims green.

**What got built, and the CDC lesson inside it:**
- **`double_buffer.py`** is where the two clock domains actually meet. Two physical framebuffers: at any instant one is being written by the DPI side (pixel clock) and the other read by the scan-out side (scan clock), *never the same one* — that's what makes the output tear-free. Only a **single toggle bit** crosses between the clocks (through a 2-flop synchronizer), and the buffer swap happens only at the scan-out's own frame boundary so a swap can never tear a frame mid-scan. This is the canonical safe-CDC pattern, and building it the minimal-crossing way is the lesson.
- The sims earned their keep twice: they caught a bug where a frame got split across both buffers (because `frame_start` coincides with the first pixel), and a second where my *test's* measurement window was zero-width. Both fixed before any hardware could be confused by them.

**Two things I recorded for when you're back at the bench** (in `INTERFACE-CONTRACT.md`): the provisional JP8 ball map for all 26 DPI signals, and two risks to resolve *with* the Pi and scope rather than guess at now — (1) the pixel clock lands on ball L18, a general I/O rather than a dedicated clock pin, so we'll verify nextpnr can route it to a clock net (fallback: feed it through a PLL), and (2) the RGB line-to-color order on Pi 5 DPI, which we'll confirm by eye at first light and pin down as a swizzle constant.

**Still cooking:** a synthesis run at *full wall geometry* (384×128, 4 chains, 10-bit, double-buffered) to confirm the project's founding bet — that the whole double buffer fits in the 85F's 3.74 Mbit of block RAM. It's synthesizing in the background; I'll report the block-RAM count when it lands.

**The board top itself** (`top_translator.py`, mapping DPI to JP8 and HUB75 to J32) I deliberately deferred — it needs your modeline live and the PCLK-clocking decision, so it's the natural first task for the bring-up session once you've got the cloned Pi, the modeline, and the Adafruit HAT wired. When you're ready, the milestone is a good one: **the panel showing the Pi's Linux console with zero rayglow code running** — the wall as a monitor.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-20T11:40:08.717Z*

The founding bet of the whole project is now confirmed with a real synthesis number, not arithmetic.

**The full wall fits the FPGA — comfortably.** One 6144×24 true-dual-port framebuffer bank synthesizes to **9 block RAMs**; the complete 384×128, 4-chain, 10-bit, double-buffered framebuffer is 16 such banks = **144 block RAMs**, plus ~12–24 for the gamma LUT, landing around **156–168 of the 85F's 208 (75–81%)**. That's the answer to the question that justified choosing the ECP5 in the first place — and note *why* it was worth measuring rather than trusting the 2.25-of-3.74-Mbit arithmetic: the real constraint is how a 24-bit-wide, dual-clock memory *packs* into fixed 18-Kbit blocks, and that could have blown the budget even though the raw bits fit. It didn't. No external RAM, ever, for this wall.

Two honest asterisks, both recorded in the ROADMAP:
- The **gamma LUT replicates per chain** (3 read ports × 8 chain-halves = 24 copies at full width). Cheap now, but if block RAM ever gets tight it moves to distributed LUT-RAM and frees ~12–24 blocks. The future 512×128 wall would be tight (~92%) and wants a re-measure before committing.
- The **full flattened design synthesizes slowly** (yosys grinds for minutes in the memory-mapping pass with 16 wide dual-clock memories) — which is why the whole-wall run stalled and I measured a single bank instead. Fine for a one-off bitstream; if it becomes annoying at Phase 3 we build per-chain.

**Net state:** Phase 0 and the Phase 2 gateware are done and committed — the entire translation layer (DPI capture → clock-domain-crossing double buffer → HUB75 scan-out) is simulation-verified end-to-end and proven to fit the silicon. What remains for Phase 2 is hardware integration, which is exactly what your current work sets up: the cloned Pi with the modeline, the Adafruit HAT buffering the panel, and then the board top wiring DPI to JP8 — culminating in the wall showing the Pi's Linux console as a plain monitor. Whenever you're back with the Pi and HAT ready, that's where we pick up, and the first task there is resolving the two flagged unknowns (the PCLK-to-clock-net routing and the RGB color order) with the scope.

*2026-07-21T02:49:28.950Z*

This is the payoff of checking the live system instead of trusting the file — **the DPI is currently failing to initialize, and I can see exactly why.** Let me answer both questions with the evidence in hand.

## Q1 — the header: you're in the right place

`JP8` on the eval board *is* the header silkscreened "RASPBERRY PI" — they're the same thing. It's a 40-pin receptacle wired straight to FPGA bank 3, and every DPI signal we need lands there (confirmed by the pin map in `INTERFACE-CONTRACT.md §4a`). So plugging the boards together at that header is correct, not a mistake. Two things to verify since you stacked them physically:
- **Pin-1 alignment.** Make sure JP8 pin 1 lines up with the Pi's 40-pin pin 1. It almost certainly does (the Pi booted and nothing released smoke — a reversed connection would put 5 V onto a signal or ground pin), but eyeball the pin-1 markers to be sure, because a reversed header would scramble the whole DPI mapping later.
- **Keep the EVN's `JP6`/`JP7` power jumpers OPEN.** Both boards are self-powered, and the EVN's bank-3 I/O rail (VCCIO3) is fed from the EVN's *own* 3.3 V by default — so the DPI inputs work without borrowing Pi power. With JP6/JP7 open, only signals and ground are shared and the two 5 V/3.3 V supplies can't fight. (If either is installed, remove it.)

## Q2 — the config: the DPI line is right, but two *other* lines are killing it

Your `vc4-kms-dpi-generic` line is correct — the modeline is internally consistent (416×140 total → 3.49 MHz → 60 Hz) and matches our contract. But the DPI is **not actually coming up**, and the Pi's kernel log says why:

```
pinctrl-rp1: pin gpio8 already requested by 1f0003c000.serial; cannot claim for 1f00148000.dpi
drm-rp1-dpi 1f00148000.dpi: Error applying setting, reverse things back
```

`dtoverlay=uart3` claims **GPIO8/GPIO9** (I confirmed with `pinctrl`: pin 8 = TXD3, pin 9 = RXD3). DPI needs *all* of GPIO0–27, GPIO8 is one of its data lines, so when DPI tries to grab GPIO8 the kernel refuses — and the DPI driver **bails out entirely** ("reverse things back"). There's no DPI DRM connector on the system right now as a result. `dtparam=spi=on` collides the same way (it errors on GPIO9 too). This is a clone dedicated to the FPGA path, so neither is needed here.

**The fix** — comment out both lines, then reboot:

```
#dtparam=spi=on
#dtoverlay=uart3
```

After reboot, `pinctrl get 0-27` should show GPIO0–27 in a DPI alt-function (`a...`) instead of `ip`/TXD3, and a DPI connector should appear under `/sys/class/drm/`.

## One more thing — geometry

Your modeline is **384×128** (the full future wall), but the bench has a single **64×32** panel. Rather than have you re-edit the modeline, I'm adding a capture-bounds gate to the translator so the FPGA stores just the **top-left 64×32** of the Pi's frame and shows that on the bench panel — enough to prove DPI works (you'll see the corner of the console), and it keeps your modeline at the final wall geometry. Let me build that now while you apply the config fix:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-21T03:32:54.020Z*

Both observations are exactly right, including your rolling-shutter intuition. Here's what's going on with the flicker, in two parts:

**The camera part is normal and not a defect.** A HUB75 panel is *never* actually "on" — it's rapidly scanning 16 address rows and, within each, flashing binary-weighted brightness planes (BCM). Your eye integrates all that into steady color, but a high-speed rolling-shutter camera slices across those plane/scan phases and catches them mid-cycle, so rows read as "occasionally not full brightness." Every LED matrix looks like this on high-speed video. The fact that it looks clear to your eye is the real verdict.

**The genuine bit** — the slight dimming concentrated on the *last rows of the top half* — is the small residual of the same ghosting we saw at first light (the old "rows 14–19" artifact). It's an OE/blanking-timing effect: when the scan advances the address lines, the previously-lit row doesn't get quite enough blanking time to fully extinguish before the next row drives, so the boundary rows lose a sliver of brightness. The buffered HAT path shrank it from "visible skew" to "camera-only shimmer." The clean fix is the **blanking-guard interval** already recorded as a scan-out v2 upgrade (`SCANOUT.md`) — assert OE a touch longer around each latch/address change. It's a small, sim-testable gateware change; I'd batch it with the other display refinements (measuring refresh, bumping to 10–12 BCM planes) rather than do it piecemeal.

**Where this leaves us:** Phase 2's ingest path is *fully validated on hardware* — DPI capture, the clock-domain crossing, gamma, geometry, and now color order all confirmed. The FPGA is a faithful HUB75 display controller taking live video from the Pi. Everything's committed; the interface contract records the confirmed pixel format and the one open item (the driver clamping `vactive` to 480, which only matters when we drive the true 384×128 wall in Phase 3).

(Housekeeping: I left the color bands in the Pi's framebuffer — the console will redraw itself the next time there's `tty1` activity, or I can clear it.)

**The payoff step from here** is getting your *actual rayglow shaders* onto the panel over DPI, not just the console — which means adding an additive `--output kms` mode to the renderer in the `~/Projects/rayglow` repo (render a frame → blit into the DPI framebuffer, PIO path untouched). That closes Phase 2's acceptance criterion and is the genuinely exciting "my shaders, through the FPGA, on the wall" moment. It's a meatier change in the *other* repo, so it's a natural next-session chunk.

Want me to dive into the rayglow `--output kms` path now, or is this a good place to pause? I can also knock out the scan-out polish (blanking guard + refresh measurement) first if you'd rather tidy the display before the software work — your call on direction.

Good division of labor. I'll work the scan-out while you read — and I've done some honest re-analysis of that flicker that changes what "polish" should mean here, which I'll fold into the docs so your read reflects reality.

First, the substantive gateware change — a **blanking-guard interval**. Let me explain the real reasoning as I build it: the current engine already blanks heavily *between* bitplanes (the whole ~131-cycle shift is blanked) and sets the row address ahead of time, so those aren't the problem. The one spot with almost no margin is **between the latch pulse and enabling OE** — currently a single cycle. If the panel's latch-to-driver output needs longer than 83 ns to settle, the very start of each row's display glitches, which is a plausible source of that boundary-row dimming. A guard there gives it defined settling time:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-21T03:43:49.327Z*

Done — the scan-out polish is complete and hardware-confirmed. Since you're reading the docs, here's what changed in them so your mental model matches `main`:

**What I did this turn (all committed, 15/15 sims green):**
- **Blanking-guard interval** — a `guard` parameter on the scan-out engine that inserts blanked settle cycles between the latch pulse and enabling OE. `guard=8` (~667 ns) is now live in the translator, and it's what turned your "occasionally dim boundary rows" into "crystal clear, no flicker." The reasoning is worth internalizing: the engine already blanks the *entire* pixel-shift (~131 cycles between planes) and sets the row address a full shift ahead, so the only under-margined moment was the single cycle between latching a row and lighting it. That's now fixed, and two new sims prove the guard changes nothing about BCM brightness (guard cycles are blanked, so lit-time is identical).
- **`SCANOUT.md`** now documents the guard, the honest "where the blanking actually is" analysis, and marks the genuinely-remaining scan-out lever: **overlap** (shifting the next plane while displaying the current one), which would roughly double refresh (~139 → ~270 Hz) by hiding the shift under display time. I deliberately deferred it — 137 Hz is already flicker-free, so it's a "if we ever want more headroom" item, not a need.
- **`ROADMAP.md`** — the flicker item is closed, refresh is recorded (137 Hz, computed exactly since the FSM has no variable latency), and brightness control is noted as already handled by the runtime `unit` knob (which the wall's all-white power cap will ride on).

**Net:** the scan-out path is now in genuinely good shape — flicker-free, gamma-correct, brightness-tunable, and cleanly parametric for the eventual 4-chain wall. Combined with the validated DPI ingest, the FPGA is a solid, faithful HUB75 display controller.

**When you surface from the docs, the two things ahead are:**
1. **The payoff — `rayglow --output kms`**: teach the renderer to scan its shaders out over DPI (a change in the `~/Projects/rayglow` repo), so your actual visuals — not the console — light up through the FPGA. That's the exciting next milestone and closes Phase 2's acceptance criterion.
2. **The `vactive=480` clamp** (Phase 3 prep): the Pi's DPI driver won't emit 128 lines, which the true 384×128 wall will need. Not urgent, but it's the one open question between here and driving the real wall.

No action needed from you right now — the panel's showing the flawless DPI feed, everything's committed. Ping me when you want to start the rayglow KMS output path (or tackle the `vactive` question first, if you'd rather clear that runway before the software work).

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-21T05:18:31.885Z*

**Your shaders are running through the FPGA right now.** `simpleinteresting` is rendering on the Pi, going out over DPI, through the ECP5, onto the bench panel — the full software path is live and confirmed working. Look at the panel: you should see the shader animating **smoothly** (60 Hz now, after the clock bump) in the top-left 64×32 corner. It's running in the background on the Pi, so take your time — tell me what you see, and I'll stop it whenever.

That closes the payoff milestone: **your renderer, your shaders, through your FPGA, on the wall** — no RP2350, no pack, no custom protocol, just video.

What I built and proved this turn:
- **`--output kms`** in the rayglow renderer (on branch `feat/output-kms`, your WIP untouched): a new `KmsOut` sink that blits the rendered frame straight to `/dev/fb0`, and a `run_kms` loop that keeps all the live machinery (control plane, `--loop`, audio feed, pausable clock) but drops the fold/pack/transport tail entirely — the FPGA does that work now. It forces resolve gamma to 1.0 so the FPGA's CIE LUT owns gamma, per the interface contract. Mutagen auto-deployed it to the Pi.
- **Bumped the DPI clock** 3.5 → 12.5 MHz so the feed refreshes at 60 Hz instead of a choppy 16.8.
- The blit is essentially free (~0 ms; it's a 49 KB memory write), and render is the only cost.

Two things worth your awareness (both in the ROADMAP):
1. **The cloned Pi is rendering on `llvmpipe` — software, not the V3D hardware GPU.** Harmless at 64×32 (119 fps), but the full 384×128 wall will need real V3D, so I've flagged it to investigate before Phase 3 — the clone likely shifted the DRM device/render-node selection when the DPI/KMS setup changed.
2. **The Pi's DPI driver still clamps to 384×480** regardless of our requested height — we're cropping the region we need, which is fine, but the true 384×128 wall geometry is the other Phase-3 runway item.

The natural next step is the one you set up: the **6× single chain (384×32)** for a full-width demo across all six panels. That needs an FPGA reconfig (translator `width=384` plus a PLL for the scan clock, since a 6×-wider shift wants more refresh/brightness headroom) and you connecting the chain. Want me to go for the full-width version next, or would you rather I chase down the llvmpipe/V3D regression first so we're not building the wider demo on software rendering? Either order works — the llvmpipe fix matters more the bigger we scale, so there's a case for clearing it before 6×.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-21T05:40:19.583Z*

Both are great catches — and #2 explains everything. `vc4-kms-v3d` is what enables the V3D *3D* driver; you overwrote it with the DPI overlay, so GL fell back to llvmpipe (the DPI display still worked because on Pi 5 that's driven by the separate RP1 DPI driver). One thing to watch: `max_framebuffers=2` plus the vc4 driver can add a second framebuffer (HDMI), which might renumber `/dev/fb*` — so I'll add a `--fbdev` option first and identify the DPI framebuffer by its 384-wide geometry after reboot, so we don't accidentally blit to the wrong device. ## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-21T05:49:09.202Z*

The kms milestone is fully in the books — excellent. Now the FPGA reconfig for the 6× chain. The key design decisions:

- **width=384, chains=1** — one electrical strip, 6 panels daisy-chained off the HAT (same J32 pins, you just chain more panels).
- **PLL scan clock (60 MHz → 30 MHz shift)** — a 6×-wider row means 6× the shift time, so at the 12 MHz clock the refresh/duty would sag (63 Hz, 34% — dim and borderline-flickery). The 60 MHz PLL brings it to ~150 Hz at ~68% duty. 30 MHz shift matches the v2 wall's own clock regime, and the Adafruit HAT buffers it to 5 V.
- **guard=40** — the blanking guard scaled to keep the same ~667 ns settle that killed the flicker at 12 MHz (it's a physical panel delay, so it needs more cycles at the faster clock).

The elegant part: this needs **zero changes** to the scan-out engine or double buffer — I just wrap the translator in a domain-rename so its whole scan-out side runs in the PLL's 60 MHz domain, while DPI capture stays in the pixel-clock domain. The Phase 0 PLL and the parametric engine pay off exactly here. Let me write it:

You should then see the shader spanning all six panels — the full 384-pixel width, top 32 rows, ~150 Hz refresh.

Two things worth a glance as you wire it up, both in your wheelhouse:
- **Power for a 6-long chain.** Six panels pull meaningfully more than one — make sure the chain has adequate 5 V and power injection along its length (a single feed at the head can brown out the far end at high brightness). `will-voidrainbow` is full-frame color, so it'll draw more than a sparse shader.
- **Signal integrity at 30 MHz over 6 panels.** The Adafruit HAT buffers to 5 V, and 30 MHz over 6-deep is within the regime your v2 wall targeted (25 MHz over 12-deep), so it should be clean — but if you see garbling or dropped pixels on the far panels, that's the tell, and the fix is a one-line PLL change to drop the shift clock (I can rebuild in a minute).

No rush — ping me when the chain's ready and I'll light it up. This is a natural spot to pause while you do the hardware.

*2026-07-21T06:20:05.180Z*

That's a textbook chain-depth signal-integrity signature, and the details you gave are diagnostic gold: **shapes/positions perfect to the last pixel** means the clock and shift are clean, so it's specifically the **data values** degrading — and "bleeding toward white" means bits that should be 0 are being sampled as partial-1s, worse with each panel. That's the data lines not settling fully at 30 MHz over a 6-deep chain (accumulated load + propagation skew). It's exactly what I flagged, and the fix is the one-line lever: **drop the shift clock**.

Committed everywhere. Here's where the project stands — and it's a genuinely big place to be.

**Milestone: the FPGA is driving a real multi-panel display with your actual shaders.** `will-voidrainbow` is rendering on the Pi's V3D, streaming out over DPI, through the ECP5, and across all six panels of a 384×32 chain as one coherent frame at ~102 Hz — clean, after dropping the shift clock to 20 MHz. Everything the translation layer was conceived to do, it now does end-to-end on hardware.

What this turn established, beyond the demo:
- **The parametric engine + PLL design paid off exactly as intended.** Going from one 64×32 panel to a six-panel 384-wide strip was a `width=384` change plus wrapping the scan-out in a 40 MHz clock domain — no engine surgery. That's the same lever that scales to the full 4-chain wall.
- **The signal-integrity behavior is now characterized.** 30 MHz shift bled color toward white per panel over a 6-deep chain; 20 MHz is clean. Your diagnostic detail (shapes perfect, only color degrading) is what pinned it to data-line settling rather than clocking — and I left a 15 MHz fallback preset staged in case a longer chain ever needs it. That data point directly informs the Phase 3 wing-board design (series termination will let you push the clock back up).
- The renderer's `--output kms` path is on branch `feat/output-kms`, your WIP untouched, mutagen auto-deploying to the Pi.

The shader's still running on the panels in the background — enjoy it; I'll stop it whenever you want.

**The runway from here to the full wall (Phase 3)** has three known items, all already scoped in the ROADMAP: merge the `feat/output-kms` branch once you're happy with it; the `vactive`-clamp question (getting the Pi to emit true 384×128 instead of 384×480-cropped) for the 4-tall wall; and the modular 2-chain wing boards with proper series termination (which is what lets the shift clock — and thus refresh/brightness — climb back up for the wider chains). But none of that is tonight's problem. Tonight, you render shaders on an FPGA-driven LED wall that didn't exist a week ago. Nicely done.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-21T09:49:10.630Z*

That's a clean review, and it settles the "chip-select" question decisively — **you don't need one, and you don't need to hand-build any CS mechanism.** Here's why, straight from your own `NET-SPEC.md`:

The HAT's four `SN74AHCT245` buffers are wired **DIR→5V, /OE→GND (§3)** — permanently-enabled, one-directional level shifters. There's no chip-select on the HUB75 side because a continuously-scanned LED wall never wants one. The "CS" you're remembering was the **Pi→RP2350 link's** chip-select (`§6`, the SPI/PIO framing on GP21/GP25) — and that entire transport is the thing the FPGA *replaces*. So for the pure level-shifting role, two HATs give you four fully-buffered chains with zero CS logic.

The one real task is physical: the HAT's inputs arrive on its `J1` socket at the (transposed) RP2350-PiZero GPIO positions, so you drive them from the ECP5 with jumpers. That's the "translation layer" you were picturing — but it's **passive wiring, not a CS circuit**, and I can make it paint-by-numbers with an exact pin map. Let me verify a couple of things and build the gateware + map. First, confirm the HAT's J1 pinout details:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-21T09:52:35.091Z*

Everything's built, documented, and committed. Here's the plan for getting onto the full wall with your existing HATs.

## The chip-select question: resolved, and in your favor

There's **nothing to hand-build.** Your HAT's `SN74AHCT245` buffers are wired permanently-enabled (DIR→5 V, /OE→GND), so they're always-on level shifters with no chip-select — which is exactly what a continuously-scanned wall wants. The "CS" you remembered was the *Pi→RP2350 link's* framing signal, and that whole transport is the thing the FPGA replaces. So two HATs give you four fully-buffered chains with zero CS logic. The "translation layer" you pictured is just **passive wiring** from the ECP5 to each HAT's `J1` socket, and I've reduced it to a lookup table.

## What's ready

- **`top_wall.py`** — the multi-chain wall driver (2 or 4 chains, 384-wide strips, in the same 20 MHz-shift PLL domain that ran your 6-panel chain clean). The 2-chain build passes timing with room to spare (scan domain 79 MHz vs 40 needed, 72 of 208 block RAMs).
- **`hardware/RAYGLOW-HAT-ADAPTER.md`** — the paint-by-numbers wiring map: every FPGA signal → its ECP5 header pin → the exact HAT `J1` pin. That J1 column already bakes in the transposed PiZero pinout (the GPIO4↔14 / 5↔15 / 9↔12 / 10↔11 swaps), so you just follow the table — no mental arithmetic on the gotcha.

## Recommended staging (one variable at a time)

1. **Stage 1 — one HAT, 2 chains (384×64, top two rows).** This validates the RayGLow-HAT interfacing and multi-chain scan-out with half the wiring. It's a new level-shifter board for us (we've only used the Adafruit HAT so far), so proving one HAT first isolates any wiring/mapping error from the second-HAT-plus-more-chains step.
2. **Stage 2 — two HATs, 4 chains (384×128, full wall).** Wire HAT #2 identically for chains 2–3, fan the 7 shared control lines to both HATs, `NUM_CHAINS=4`, done.

Your hands-on part for Stage 1: decouple the top two rows into two separate 6-panel chains, wire one HAT per the adapter table, feed its `J5` from the panel 5 V, and common the grounds (ECP5 ↔ HAT ↔ PSU, single star). Two reminders from the doc: **the HAT's `J5` takes panel 5 V, never the ECP5**, and data lines aren't series-terminated on this board — so if you see the color-bleed-to-white creeping in over a chain, that's the tell and I drop the shift clock.

When you've got one HAT wired, tell me and I'll build/load `NUM_CHAINS=2`, restart the renderer at 384×64, and we light up the top half of the wall. Nice thing about this path: you'll have the whole wall displaying shaders through the FPGA **before** you invest a minute in a custom PCB — and by then we'll know exactly what the wing board needs to improve (mainly that data-line termination for a higher clock).

*2026-07-22T16:29:30.761Z*

Good — let's light up the top two chains. First, the answer you asked for:

## Your reload command

```fish
openFPGALoader -b ecp5_evn ~/Projects/rayglow-fpga/build/top.bit
```

That's it — it re-writes `build/top.bit` into the ECP5's SRAM in ~2 seconds. `-b ecp5_evn` is the board preset (tells it which FTDI/JTAG config). It works from any directory since I gave the absolute path, and `openFPGALoader` is already on your fish PATH from the Phase 0 setup. One caveat: `build/top.bit` is always *whatever I built last*, so after this session it holds the 2-chain wall bitstream — reload that and you're back where we are. (The permanent fix is Phase 4: writing the bitstream to the board's SPI flash so it boots itself with no host — I'll set that up once the wall's proven, and then power cycles just work.)

Now, building and loading the 2-chain wall, then restarting the renderer at 384×64:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-22T20:56:15.591Z*

So glad the two chains look awesome — and there's a lot of good thinking in your message. Let me take the three things in turn, and I'll stage the 4-chain build while you finish HAT #2.

## Orientation: software flip, definitely not a hardware toggle

Don't build a hardware toggle — it would add gateware logic and a failure point for zero benefit. The rayglow renderer already has exactly the right mechanism, and it's **free**: the GPU resolve pass bakes orientation flips in via `config.FLIP_H` / `config.FLIP_V` (that config field exists precisely for "panels mounted inverted vs the scan convention"). Baked into the resolve shader means **zero added latency, no extra hardware, no failure points** — strictly better than flipping every shader, too. Since you'll run this orientation 95% of the time, set it once and forget it. I'll set both flags for a 180° rotation as the starting guess:

## The CAT6a twisted-pair idea: yes, good instinct — here's how I'd prioritize it

Your plan is sound, and CLK is exactly the right signal to focus on:

- **CLK is the one that matters.** It toggles at the 20 MHz pixel rate — the fastest shared signal by far. A twisted pair gives it a tight, adjacent ground return, shrinking the current loop so ringing/crosstalk drop. That's the single most effective thing you can do for jumper SI. Definitely twist CLK.
- **LAT, OE, and the address lines change at *row* rate (~kHz), not pixel rate** — they update once per 384-pixel row, so they're ~1000× slower than CLK and essentially immune to this. Twisting LAT/OE is fine belt-and-suspenders since you have CAT6a to burn, but it's optional; the address lines (A–D) don't need it at all.
- **Your topology is right:** star-split at the FPGA CLK pin (both pairs' signals meet there), each branch a twisted signal+ground to its HAT. Star-from-source beats a daisy-chain stub. Ground each pair at **both** ends — at the FPGA on a header GND pin next to the CLK pin, and at each HAT on the J1 GND pin next to that HAT's CLK input (J1 pin 36 has GND neighbors). Keeps the loop small end to end.
- **On the "terminations set up for pairs":** you're reading J32/J33 right — they're bank-7 **differential-pair** breakouts, and there *are* on-board termination footprints per pair, but they're **DNI (unpopulated) by default** (Fig A.8). So today the pins are plain single-ended and your twisted pair just supplies the return — no termination interaction to worry about. If CLK ever rings after twisting, two escalations, in order: (1) a 22–33 Ω series resistor at the FPGA CLK source *before* the split, or (2) populate one of those DNI pair-termination footprints. You almost certainly won't need either at 20 MHz with a good return.

Net: twist CLK (worth it), optionally LAT/OE (cheap insurance), skip it for the address lines, star from the source, ground both ends. I dropped a matching note and a command quick-reference into the adapter doc:
