## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-22T21:17:40.920Z*

Confirmed — it dies in **`MEMORY_LIBMAP`** (the pass that maps inferred memories to physical block-RAM cells), and it's not the sandbox. The culprit is the gamma LUT: I wrote it as *one* memory with a read port per chain-half-channel — 12 ports at 2 chains (which mapped fine), but **24 ports at 4 chains**, and MEMORY_LIBMAP's port-combination search blows up superlinearly. The fix is minimal and low-risk: give each read port its own small LUT memory, so the mapper sees 24 trivial ROMs instead of one 24-port monster. Same gamma function, so the sims are unaffected:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-22T21:18:29.835Z*

Root cause fixed and rebuilding. To recap the diagnosis chain: the 4-chain build wasn't a sandbox cap or a real OOM — it was yosys's memory-mapping pass choking on my gamma LUT, which I'd written as a single memory with a read port per chain-half-channel. That's 24 read ports at 4 chains, and the mapper's port-combination search blows up superlinearly (12 ports at 2 chains was fine, which is why we never saw it until now). Splitting it into 24 independent little ROMs makes each map trivially. Sims still pass (identical gamma), and the 4-chain build is running in the background.

I'll report the fit and timing the moment it lands — the number I'm watching is total block RAM, since the LUTs now cost some EBR on top of the 144 framebuffer blocks (should still fit the 208, and yosys may pack the little ROMs into distributed LUT-RAM instead). Once it's confirmed and HAT #2 is wired, we load it and light all four rows. No rush on your end — keep at the header rework.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T01:06:17.043Z*

A week of board rework, a fried '245 hunted down across three rewires — and now the full 384×128 wall rendering shaders at 120 fps. That's the project's summit, congratulations. Both remaining artifacts you noticed are diagnosable from here, and one of them is my code's fault, so let's fix it properly. Then flash boot, which kills your power-cycle backtracking for good.

**The tearing is the Pi side, not the FPGA.** The FPGA's double-buffer swaps only at frame boundaries (sim-proven tear-free). But my `KmsOut.blit()` writes into `/dev/fb0` **asynchronously to the DPI scanout** — at 120 fps against a 60 Hz scanout, half your blits land mid-scan, and the scanout reads a half-old/half-new frame. That's your fast-motion tearing, and likely some of the faint flicker too. The fix is to pace blits to the display's vertical blank via DRM's `WAIT_VBLANK` — blit lands during blanking, before the scanout re-reads row 0. Let me implement it:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T01:12:35.371Z*

Everything's committed. Here's the state of play after this turn:

## Done just now

**Tearing: fixed, live on the wall right now.** The cause was mine, not yours — `KmsOut` was blitting into the framebuffer at 120 fps with zero synchronization against the 60 Hz DPI scanout, so half the blits landed mid-scan and the FPGA faithfully displayed torn frames. The fix: each blit now waits for the display's **vertical blank** (`DRM_IOCTL_WAIT_VBLANK`) and lands during blanking, before the scanout re-reads row 0. Side effects, both good: the render loop is now locked to 60 fps (no more rendering frames nobody sees), and the renderer finds the right DRM card by matching sysfs parent devices — necessary, since your card numbering has been shuffling across reboots (it's card0 today, was card1 last week). `will-apollo` is running with it now: **check fast motion — the tearing should be gone.** And once you've looked: **is the faint flicker still there?** The tearing shimmer may have been part of it.

**Bookkeeping:** Phase 3 closed out (with your '245 saga recorded — that hunt deserved documentation), wing boards formally parked with a status banner on the design doc, orientation flips committed, memory updated.

## Blocked on one jumper: flash boot

The flash write cleanly fails at exactly the flash-access step while JTAG works perfectly — which points at **JP18**, the "Flash Chip Select" jumper (UG Fig 6.2: it bridges the FPGA's CS pin to the flash chip; open jumper = flash unreachable). When you're next at the board:

1. **Install/verify JP18** (near the flash chip, per the Fig 1.3 jumper map), then tell me and I'll rewrite the flash (~a minute; the wall blinks out and I restore it).
2. Then set **SW1 for MSPI boot: position 2 ON, position 3 OFF, position 4 ON** and power-cycle — the FPGA should configure *itself* from flash (green DONE without any host command). If it doesn't, my ON=0 polarity read on SW1 is inverted and we flip it — no risk either way, JTAG always works regardless of the switches.
3. One honest caveat, recorded in the ROADMAP: the design's PLL reference is the 12 MHz FTDI clock, which only runs while the **mini-USB is plugged into a powered host**. So after flash boot, keep the EVN's USB plugged in — ideally **into the Pi**, which also sets up reflash-over-SSH. True no-USB standalone needs migrating the clock to the 200 MHz oscillator (an LVDS-input question I've deferred deliberately).

## Next after that

If the flicker survives the vsync fix, the proper cure is the **overlap upgrade** — the one remaining scan-out lever, hiding the pixel-shift under the display interval: refresh goes ~102 → ~145 Hz at *identical* brightness (the cheap alternative, halving `unit`, costs ~⅓ of the brightness). It's a meaty, sim-first gateware change and the natural next build. Beyond that, Phase 4's menu is brightness control, 120 Hz DPI, and — the one I'm quietly excited about — the Phase 5 audio-DSP experiment.

Tell me: tearing gone? flicker status? And once JP18 is in, we make the wall survive a power cycle for the first time.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T01:38:51.301Z*

That photo is diagnostic gold, and combined with the 16-row-structured banding it points at something important: **this tear is mine, in the FPGA's double-buffer CDC — and my earlier "tear-free by design" claim was wrong.** Your race-condition instinct is exactly right, just one level up from the ABCD lines. Here's the actual mechanism:

The writer starts overwriting a buffer at the *first pixel* of each new DPI frame, and fires the swap notice at the same moment — but the scan-out only swaps at its own frame boundary, up to ~9.8 ms later. During that window the panel is **displaying the very buffer being overwritten**. And because the scan doesn't run top-to-bottom over the wall (each address lights 8 rows at once — one per 16-row half-block), while the DPI write *does* run top-to-bottom, the read and write pointers leapfrog each other and slice the frame into alternating old/new bands with exactly the 16-row periodic structure in your photo. The sim never caught it because my capstone test fed *identical* frames every time — deliberately insensitive to exactly this bug.

The fix is elegant and needs no extra memory: fire the swap notice **when the last captured row completes** (~27% into the DPI frame, since the 480-line mode idles after our 128 rows) instead of at the next frame's start. The reader then always swaps during the writer's idle gap — provably disjoint. Sim-first discipline: I'll write the tearing-catching test *first*, prove it fails on the current code, then fix:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T01:44:50.751Z*

The fix is on the wall — **go make some noise at it.** Drive `will-rayballs` hard with audio and watch fast horizontal motion; the interlaced banding from your photo should be gone entirely.

What your photo caught, because it's a genuinely good bug: my "tear-free by design" double buffer had two stacked flaws, and your description — "a race condition between the ABCD signals and blocks of lines rendering too early or too late" — was almost literally correct:

1. **The swap notice fired too late.** The writer announced a completed frame at the *next* frame's first pixel — the exact moment it began overwriting the other buffer. The scan-out only acts on that notice at its own frame boundary, up to ~9.8 ms later, so every DPI frame had a window where the panel was displaying the buffer being written. And since the scan lights one row per 16-row half-block simultaneously while the DPI write fills top-to-bottom, the read/write pointers leapfrog — slicing the frame into those alternating 16-row-pitch bands. That's your photo.
2. Fixing #1 exposed a second, subtler layer: with the notice moved, the writer/reader **buffer phase was off by one** — each frame landed in the buffer the reader had just started showing. The fix is which buffer the very first frame lands in.

The repair moves the swap notice to **capture-complete** (~4.3 ms into each DPI frame — the 480-line mode idles for ~12.4 ms after our 128 rows, and the reader always swaps inside that idle tail), plus the phase-matched init. And the process note I take on the chin: my original capstone sim fed *identical frames* every cycle, which is precisely blind to tearing. The new regression test streams distinct-tagged frames at the real timing ratio, reproduced both bug layers exactly, and now passes — 16/16.

One honest constraint recorded in the docs: this handoff is safe while the scan frame (9.8 ms) fits in the writer's idle tail (12.4 ms). If we ever get the Pi to emit a *true* 384×128 mode (the `vactive` clamp question), that margin shrinks and the handoff needs revisiting — the two issues are now formally linked in the ROADMAP.

So, two questions when you've had a look:
1. **Fast motion clean now?** (Both tear sources — Pi blit and FPGA handoff — are fixed.)
2. **Is the faint flicker gone too?** There's a real chance it was low-grade frame-mixing from this same bug. If it survives, the overlap upgrade (~102 → ~145 Hz at identical brightness) is the next gateware build and I'm ready to start it.

And still queued from last turn whenever you're at the board: **JP18** for the flash-boot writing, then the SW1 switches — that's the one that ends the reload-after-power-cycle dance for good.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T02:23:40.770Z*

Good testing, and the new photo actually tells me something: the banding is *finer* than before (the 16-row scan-structure bands are gone), which says the FPGA fix landed — but a second, Pi-side tear source is still active, and your "framerate drop" observation is the clue that ties it together. The vsync'd loop I built has almost zero slack: it measured `render 2.4 ms + blit+wait 13.9 ms ≈ 16.3 ms` against a 16.7 ms budget — so any jitter (Python GC, the audio thread you've now enabled) makes it miss a vblank, which is both your stutter *and* a late blit landing mid-scanout, i.e. a tear. The current code also converts the frame *after* the vblank wait, wasting the tight blanking window (only ~670 µs) on numpy work.

Three things this turn: retry the flash (you reseated JP18 — worth an immediate try), fix + instrument the blit timing, and answer your JTAG/Pi questions. Flash first:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T02:45:44.193Z*

Everything's committed and the wall is running on the new backend. The full picture:

## The tearing: third layer found, and this one was the real one

Your instrumented numbers exposed it: the framebuffer write took 0.02 ms — that's cached-RAM speed, impossibly fast for scanout memory. **`/dev/fb0` is not the display** — it's the kernel's fbdev *emulation*: a shadow buffer that a kernel worker copies to the real scanout buffer in 4 KB chunks (≈2.7 rows each) on its own schedule. All my careful vblank pacing was synchronizing writes into a buffer nobody scans out; the worker's chunked copies raced the beam regardless — hence the fine 2–3-row shear bands in your photo, and hence why the artifact survived both earlier fixes (which were real bugs too, just not the last one).

**The fix now live:** rayglow talks to DRM/KMS directly — it takes DRM master, owns two hardware framebuffers, and asks the display controller to **page-flip at vblank**. Tearing is impossible by construction (the flip is atomic in hardware), the flip-complete event paces the loop with a full frame of slack (zero missed flips measured), and as a bonus the console's blinking cursor can no longer write onto the wall. **Please beat on it with fast audio-driven motion** — this should finally be clean, and I'd bet the faint flicker went with it.

On the **framerate feel**: some of what you sensed was real (occasional missed vblanks in the old zero-slack loop — now gone), but part is that a clean 60 can *look* different from a torn 120, because torn frames smuggle in half-updates. If you still want it smoother after judging the clean version, the genuine levers are on my list below.

## Flash boot: the chip never heard us — check the *other* J18

The verbose write attempt read the flash's JEDEC ID as `0xFFFFFF` — silence on the bus. The tool is fine; the chip-select isn't reaching the flash. And I think I know why: you checked "**J18**" — but plain `J18` is one of the **SERDES SMA connectors** in the array across the top of the board. The one we need is **JP18**: a small 2-pin shunt jumper on the *right side*, near the flash chip (U4, the little SOIC by the config DIP switches SW1). When you get a chance, look there for the `JP18` silkscreen and make sure a shunt is actually bridging it — then I'll rerun the write. SW1: harmless to flip whenever (boot mode only matters at power-on), but don't expect a self-booting wall until the flash write actually succeeds.

## Your JTAG/Pi question — untangled

The DPI pins and JTAG are completely separate worlds. `pinctrl` shows GPIO0–27 as DPI because that's the *Pi's* 40-pin header feeding video to JP8. **JTAG never touches those pins** — it lives on the EVN's FT2232H USB chip, reached through the **mini-USB jack (J2)**, which currently runs to your desktop (that's how every `openFPGALoader` call in this project has worked). "Reflash from the Pi" just means: move that mini-USB cable from the desktop to one of the Pi's USB-A ports and install `openFPGALoader` on the Pi (`sudo apt install openfpgaloader`). Nothing about the 40-pin/DPI side changes; the Pico/PMOD rig was only ever the debug *serial console*, not programming, and stays optional. Side benefit: the FT2232H stays powered by the Pi, which keeps the 12 MHz clock (our PLL reference) alive — a prerequisite for flash boot being useful.

## Pushing clock / framerate / bit depth — the honest lever list

All on the roadmap, roughly in value order: **(1) the overlap upgrade** (shift hidden under display: ~102 → ~145 Hz refresh at identical brightness — pure gateware, sim-first, ready to build); **(2) 120 Hz DPI** (double the pixel clock and halve vtotal — the renderer's page-flips then pace at 120 fps; genuinely smoother motion); **(3) B=12 bit depth** (smoother darks; costs row time, pairs well with overlap); **(4) higher shift clock** — the one that's *hardware*-bound: 20 MHz is the unterminated-HAT ceiling we measured, so 30+ MHz is the actual argument for reviving the wing boards someday, with series termination on the data lines.

Tell me what the wall looks like under fast motion now — and if it's clean, the overlap build is my next move.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T04:58:43.808Z*

That photo narrows it beautifully — this is a *third, different* artifact class: **single-row streaks**, 1–2 pixels tall, horizontally displaced. Buffer-swap bugs (both fixed ones) produce multi-row *bands*; isolated corrupted rows point at the **DPI capture itself glitching on individual lines**. And there's a smoking gun in the Pi's own mode report: `+CK` — the RP1 drives pixel data on the **rising** clock edge… which is the same edge my capture samples on. At 3.5 MHz that mattered nothing (286 ns of slack); at the 12.5 MHz we bumped to, the data transition lands right at our sampling edge, and marginal rows glitch — a missed DE count shifts a row horizontally, exactly your streaks. The fix is textbook: **sample on the falling edge** (mid-eye). I'll also build in a permanent diagnostic — a "malformed line" detector latched onto an LED — so this failure class is observable instead of theoretical, now and at any future clock speed.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T05:04:46.177Z*

Big turn — two breakthroughs. Here's the state:

## 1. The tearing: falling-edge capture is live, with a built-in verdict light

Your photo's single-row streaks pointed away from buffer swaps entirely (those make multi-row *bands*) and at the DPI capture glitching individual lines. The design-level smoking gun: the Pi's mode reports `+CK`, meaning the RP1 **drives pixel data on the rising clock edge** — the *same edge* my capture sampled on. At the original 3.5 MHz there was tons of slack; at the 12.5 MHz we moved to, sampling lands right at the data transition and marginal rows glitch — a mis-counted DE shifts a row sideways, exactly your streaks. It also explains "less but still present": we've genuinely peeled two real layers (FPGA swap, fbdev shadow), and this was the third underneath.

The build now on the wall samples on the **falling edge** — mid-eye, half a period of margin on each side — and adds something I want to exist forever: **LED D11 latches if even one malformed line is ever captured.** So the check is now objective. Please look at two things: **is the motion clean, and is D11 dark?** Dark D11 + clean motion = capture proven healthy, and that LED stays our canary when we later push the pixel clock for 120 Hz. (If D11 lights, the phase fix wasn't enough and I escalate to ribbon-ground/clock-margin work — but I don't expect that.)

## 2. Flash boot: solved, and JP18 was innocent all along

You were right to push on this. The moment your SW1 flip took effect, the flash answered — `JEDEC 0xC22018`, the Macronix chip, loud and clear — and **the full wall bitstream wrote to flash, 100%, verified**. The root cause was subtle and worth knowing: the JTAG→SPI passthrough only reaches the flash when the **CFGMDN mode is MSPI** — in the old mode, the FPGA's config logic never connects the SPI port pins to the chip. Your jumper was always fine; the DIP switches were the gate. (Your sysCONFIG TN drop was well-aimed — that's exactly the document that behavior lives in, now filed in `.reference/ecp5/`.)

**So: the power-cycle test is armed.** Whenever you like, pull power on the EVN and restore it. Expected: green DONE comes up on its own within a second, and the wall resumes without anyone typing a reload command — the end of the backtracking that's been costing you all week. Only requirement: the mini-USB stays plugged into a powered host (the FTDI's 12 MHz feeds our PLL). If it *doesn't* self-boot, my ON=0 switch-polarity reading is inverted — flip all three SW1 positions to their opposites and cycle again.

## Then the fun list

With tearing (hopefully) closed and the board self-booting, the four upgrades are queued in the order I'd build them: **overlap** (~102 → ~145 Hz refresh, same brightness — pure gateware, biggest visual win), **120 Hz DPI** (now respectable thanks to the falling-edge margin + D11 canary), **B=12**, and the **shift-clock push** (your clean-signal observation post-HAT agrees it's plausible — D11's cousin on the output side would be the wing-board termination question).

Report back on the three: streaks, D11, and the power-cycle boot.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T05:26:49.816Z*

Excellent testing — and the D11 detail you caught ("trips on the first visible frame") exposes a flaw in my diagnostic, which reshapes everything: **the latch never clears and isn't armed-gated**, so the *guaranteed* partial line the FPGA sees when it configures mid-stream (or when the DPI signal first appears) trips it instantly and forever. Lit-D11 currently proves nothing about steady state. And since the falling-edge change didn't kill the streaks, I need discriminating data, not another guess. Also — flash boot working is a big milestone banked; USB-on-desktop is fine indefinitely (it just needs *a* powered host).

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T05:30:21.091Z*

The diagnostic build is live on the wall **and** in flash (so power cycles keep it). Here's the state and what your eyes need to answer:

## What just changed

- **Your D11 report exposed my diagnostic's flaw, not the answer.** The v1 latch armed at power-on and never cleared — so the *guaranteed* partial line the FPGA sees when it first acquires the DPI signal tripped it instantly, exactly matching your "lights on first visible frame." It was a false positive by construction. Good catch on the timing detail; it's what gave it away.
- **v2 diagnostics now running:** the checker arms only after the first VSYNC (startup partials ignored), and — the important part — it *discriminates*: **D10 latches on SHORT lines** (a DE glitch splits a row in two — the missed-clock class), **D11 latches on LONG lines** (extra clock counts — the PCLK-ringing class), and **D9 blinks for ~0.4 s on every bad line**, so you can see the error *rate*: solid ≈ constant errors, occasional flicker ≈ rare, dark ≈ clean.
- **A candidate fix rode along:** `HYSTERESIS=ON` (Schmitt-trigger input) on the PCLK and DE pins. If ribbon ringing at 12.5 MHz is the culprit, hysteresis filters the double-edges at the pad — it may have outright fixed the streaks.
- Also confirmed meanwhile: the Pi's DPI transmitter reports **zero underruns** — the corruption is definitively on our side of the wire. And your flash-boot testing means all of this survives power cycles now.

## What to report (with motion running, fast audio-driven stuff)

1. **Streaks** — gone, reduced, or unchanged?
2. **D9** — dark, occasional blink, or solid?
3. **D10 vs D11** — which (if either) latches?

The readings map to next moves: **all dark + no streaks** → hysteresis fixed it, done, and the LEDs stay as permanent canaries for the 120 Hz push. **D11/long lines** → ringing survives hysteresis → I lower the Pi's DPI pad drive strength / we improve ribbon grounding. **D10/short lines** → DE-edge trouble → likely the same treatment, or sampling. **Streaks with all LEDs dark** — the most interesting outcome — means lines arrive *bit-perfect in length* and the corruption is elsewhere (data-line skew, or something in my fold I'd then re-audit with fresh eyes). Whatever you see, the next step is data-driven rather than another educated guess — and if it comes to it, the endgame architecture (oversampling the DPI bus from a fast internal clock, treating PCLK as data instead of a clock — immune to all of this by construction) is a rewrite I'm prepared to do.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T06:08:12.085Z*

Your intuition is very likely **right** — and your three-exposure experiment is genuinely excellent methodology, because the **long exposure is the verdict**: it integrates many complete refreshes, so if rows were being captured with *wrong content*, they'd show as displaced content there too. They don't — the long exposure is flawless. Combined with your armed line-checkers staying dark indefinitely (every captured line provably exactly 384 pixels), we now have two independent proofs that **the data path is clean end to end**. (And the power-cycle LED behavior is normal: during the ~2 s flash-boot configuration all pins float with weak pull-ups, which lights the active-low LEDs until the design takes over.)

What remains is exactly what you guessed: **the physics of a multiplexed scan being watched by a camera (or a tracking eye) during fast motion**. The mechanism, concretely:

- The panel never shows a whole frame at once — the scan sweeps 16 addresses over ~9.8 ms, and each address lights **eight separate 16-row blocks simultaneously** (one per half-panel). Your short exposure froze precisely that.
- A medium exposure (~1/100 s) straddles two scan frames, which during motion show two *different* 60 Hz source frames — so the photo interleaves old/new content **with 16-row periodicity**. That's indistinguishable from "tearing" on camera, but no buffer ever tore.
- The eye does a milder version when tracking fast edges, aggravated by the awkward **102 Hz scan vs 60 Hz source cadence**: each source frame is shown for alternately one or two scan frames — uneven motion sampling reads as edge break-up.

There's a clean decisive test, and I've set it up: I'm restarting the renderer with the **control plane enabled** so you can freeze the content mid-motion — if the streaks vanish *the instant* motion stops (while the scan keeps scanning), it's confirmed presentation physics, not corruption:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T06:09:42.012Z*

Everything's committed. Now the decisive test is in your hands — the renderer is running with the control plane on, so you can freeze the *content* while the scan keeps scanning:

```fish
# freeze mid-motion (run while music is driving fast movement):
ssh rpi5 "cd ~/rayglow && uv run python tools/rayglow_ctl.py pause"
# resume:
ssh rpi5 "cd ~/rayglow && uv run python tools/rayglow_ctl.py play"
```

**If the streaks vanish the instant the image freezes** — same scan, same panels, same wiring, only motion removed — the reclassification is confirmed: what's left was never corruption, it's the physics of watching a multiplexed scan move.

Since you asked how it maths out, here are your three photos in numbers. The scan sweeps 16 addresses in **9.79 ms** (102.1 Hz), each address lighting eight separate 16-row blocks at once. Your short exposure (~1 ms) froze about a tenth of one sweep — those diagonal slabs are the addresses lit during that slice. Your long exposure (~1/15 s or longer) integrated six-plus complete sweeps of *correct, stable* content — hence flawless. And the medium exposure (~1/100 s ≈ 10 ms) almost always straddles the boundary between two scan frames — which, at 60 Hz source on a 102 Hz scan, frequently means **two different source frames interleaved at the 16-row scan structure**. During fast motion those two frames have visibly different content: photographed "tearing" with no torn buffer anywhere. The eye gets a milder dose of the same thing, aggravated by the uneven 102/60 cadence (each source frame lives for one *or* two scan frames, irregularly). This is exactly why filming commercial LED walls requires shutter-synced cameras — your wall has simply joined that club.

The honest bottom line on this long hunt: we fixed **three real bugs** (the FPGA swap protocol, the fbdev shadow copies, and marginal clock-edge sampling — each provably real, each now regression-tested or LED-monitored), and the residue is a display-physics artifact that every scanned LED wall shares. It can't be "fixed," but it **can be substantially reduced**, and the reducers are already our roadmap:

1. **The overlap upgrade** — shifts hidden under display time. At your current brightness setting it lands around **147 Hz at *higher* duty** (brighter, not dimmer), and the skew window per sweep shrinks proportionally. This is the big one, pure gateware, sim-first.
2. **Cadence tuning** — with overlap in place, dialing `unit` puts the scan near an even 2× of the source rate, removing the 1-vs-2-frame judder.
3. **120 Hz DPI** (later) — halves the motion step between source frames, the largest perceptual improvement for fast shaders, now credible thanks to the falling-edge + hysteresis margin and the D9–D11 canaries.

Run the freeze test and tell me what you see — and if it confirms, I'll start building the overlap engine.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T06:32:39.013Z*

That cross-chain observation is the strongest evidence yet — better than my LED latches, honestly. The streaks landing at **identical y-positions in all four chains simultaneously** is the fingerprint of the *shared scan* (all chains sweep addresses in lockstep off the same ADDR/LAT/OE), and it's something data corruption physically couldn't do: four electrically independent data paths don't coordinate their errors. Case closed on corruption; the presentation-physics diagnosis stands confirmed. (And no worries on the pause quirk — your observation made the test unnecessary.)

So let's build the mitigation: **the overlap engine**. And the math turns out beautifully — let me show you before I build, because at your current settings the numbers land almost perfectly:

Today (sequential), each plane costs `shift(771) + guard(41) + display(16·2^b)` serially → 24,478 cycles/row → **102.1 Hz at 66.9% duty**. With overlap, the shift of plane b+1 runs *during* plane b's display, so each plane costs `max(shift, display)`: the six LSB planes are shift-bound (~811 each), the four MSB planes display-bound → **~20,390 cycles/row → 122.6 Hz at 80.3% duty**. Three wins at once: ~20% faster scan (smaller skew window), **brighter** (duty up 13 points), and — the luck — 122.6 Hz ≈ **2.04× your 60 Hz source**, so nearly every source frame is shown exactly twice: the uneven 1-vs-2 cadence judder mostly disappears. Building it sim-first:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T06:41:37.863Z*

**The overlap engine is live on the wall — and in flash, so it survives power cycles.** Go look, because this one you should be able to *see*, twice over:

1. **It's brighter.** Duty went 66.9% → 80.3% — the panels now spend the time they used to waste blanked-while-shifting actually lit. About 20% more light at identical color math (the golden sims prove every pixel's lit-time ratio is untouched).
2. **Motion should be noticeably calmer.** Refresh went 102.1 → **122.6 Hz**, which lands at 2.04× your 60 Hz source — so instead of frames irregularly showing for one-or-two scan sweeps (the judder), nearly every frame now displays exactly twice. And each sweep is ~17% shorter, shrinking the skew window your camera and eye were catching.

What shipped underneath: the engine now loads the panels' input shift registers with plane b+1 *while* the output latches display plane b — the defining trick of serious HUB75 drivers, and the reason the panel architecture has a latch stage at all. Each plane now costs `max(shift, display)` instead of their sum: the six cheap LSB planes hide entirely under the shift, the four expensive MSB planes hide the shift entirely. The implementation splits the engine into a shift-side tracker (which row/plane is being loaded — drives the framebuffer reads and gamma bit-select) and a display-side tracker (which plane the latches hold — drives the address pins and OE duration), with the latch strobe promoting one to the other. The frame pulse moved to the wrap-latch so the double-buffer swap happens while the *old* frame's last plane is still displaying from the panel latches — provably gap-covered by the guard interval. The proven sequential engine remains in the codebase behind the default, the golden-model tests now run against *both* engines (pulse-to-pulse accumulation, exact lit-time in each), and a dedicated test asserts overlap is actually faster. 20/20 green, timing closes with 2× margin.

Given your framing — that the effect was already subtle and "even mitigating it a little would make a big difference" — a 17% shorter skew window, an even 2× cadence, *and* a brightness bump should be a very satisfying combined step. The remaining lever if you ever want more is **120 Hz DPI** (halves the motion step itself; the falling-edge capture + hysteresis + your D9–D11 canaries make the 25 MHz pixel clock a credible next experiment), and beyond that **B=12** for smoother darks.

Tell me how it looks — both the brightness jump and whether the fast-motion streaking has receded to your satisfaction. If yes, this is a natural point to also merge the rayglow `feat/output-kms` branch to your mainline, since the renderer side has proven itself thoroughly by now.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T18:36:53.822Z*

All three asks are done, and the wall is running the new build right now (it self-booted from flash after the write — you may have seen it blink dark for the ~minute of flashing, then return on its own).

## What landed

**1. Merge:** `feat/output-kms` is merged into `wall-v2-384x128` — the DPI/KMS output path (DRM page-flips, vsync, `--fbdev`/`--kms-backend`) is now mainline rayglow. Your uncommitted preset/KiCad WIP was untouched.

**2. Pi-hosted flashing, vetted end-to-end:** the desktop has provably lost the board (JTAG open fails there now), the Pi sees the FT2232H, `openfpgaloader` 0.13.1 is installed with a udev rule, `--detect` reads the ECP5 cleanly — and I did a full **build-on-desktop → `scp` → flash-from-Pi → auto-boot** cycle as the deployment for item 3. The wall + Pi are now a fully self-contained appliance: video over the header, programming over USB, flash boot on power-up, and the FTDI's 12 MHz (our PLL reference) permanently powered by the Pi. The command quick-reference in `RAYGLOW-HAT-ADAPTER.md` is updated to the new flow.

**3. Bit depth + brightness:** the wall now runs **11 BCM planes** at the same total brightness as before — meaning the darkest gradient steps are **2× finer**. Where you'd see it: slow fades to black and dim smooth gradients (the shadertoy-style vignettes) should band noticeably less. I went 11 rather than 12 deliberately: at constant brightness every extra plane costs refresh (each added LSB is a full shift slot), and 12 would also have left the brightness knob only 4 steps below default — 11 keeps ~118 Hz at a ~1.97× source cadence. And the **brightness knob is now hardware**: `SW5` switches 1–4 form a 4-bit value — **all OFF = today's brightness**, any other pattern sets it from 1 (~12%, refresh rises to ~137 Hz) to 15 (~190%, refresh drops to ~72 Hz). Takes effect within a frame, works with the Pi off, survives power cycles. Try flipping position 4 alone (= 8 = same as default) vs position 1 alone (= 1, deep dim) to feel the range.

## Where the roadmap stands

Phase 4 is nearly swept: flash boot ✓, self-contained reflash ✓, bit depth ✓, brightness ✓, and the tearing saga closed with the overlap engine. What remains is genuinely optional polish: **120 Hz DPI** (the last big motion-quality lever — halves the source frame step; the capture margins and your D9–D11 canaries are ready for the 25 MHz pixel clock), **temporal dithering** (simulated 12th+ bit without the refresh cost), the **X2 200 MHz standalone clock** (only matters if the Pi's USB ever goes away), and diagnostics readout. And then there's the one I've been saving: **Phase 5 — the audio-DSP experiment**, mic → FFT in fabric → the FPGA as an alternate feed-v3 sender. Your call on what, and whether, next — the wall itself is, by any reasonable definition, *done*: it renders your shaders, survives power cycles, has a brightness knob, and needs nothing but 5 V and a Pi.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T20:40:45.531Z*

The v1 comparison you just made is the decisive data point: **v1 ran at ~430 Hz refresh and was clean; we're at ~118 Hz.** Your eye is doing exactly the analysis my instruments couldn't — this is the multiplexed-scan artifact scaling with refresh, and "faster helps" + "16-row groups" + "least tearing at default brightness" (which is the fastest of the bright settings) all point the same way. The OE timing itself is cycle-exact (sim-proven), but the *structure* — how rarely each row gets its burst of light — is 3.6× worse than what v1 taught your eyes to expect. So let's attack refresh directly, with two levers. First, your PLL question, because it *is* the lever:

**PLL = phase-locked loop.** You couldn't find it in the eval-board PDF because it's a *silicon* feature, not a board feature — it's documented in the **family datasheet** (`FPGA-DS-02012`, the "sysCLOCK PLL" sections; the `FT_VPLL` hits you found are just the FTDI chip's own internal PLL supply pin). The ECP5 has 4 of them. Conceptually: a PLL compares a reference clock against a divided-down copy of an internal oscillator (VCO) and servos the VCO until they lock — giving you `f_out = f_ref × (feedback divider) / (input divider)`, with the constraint that the VCO must land in 400–800 MHz. That's how our 12 MHz board clock becomes the 40 MHz scan clock: `gateware/pll.py` wraps the ECP5's `EHXPLLL` primitive with divider values computed by the toolchain's `ecppll` calculator (12 × 10 / 3 = 40, VCO at 600). The scan clock is *the* refresh knob, because refresh = scan-clock ÷ cycles-per-frame.

**Lever 1 — scan clock 40 → 60 MHz: refresh 117.9 → 176.9 Hz** at identical brightness and duty. The precedent is strong: these same RayGLow HATs ran v1 at **37.5 MHz** shift over 4-panel chains cleanly; this asks 30 MHz over 6 — and you have the D9–D11 canaries plus the known color-bleed signature if it's too much.

**Lever 2 — distribute the planes.** Right now each row gets *all* its light in one burst per frame (all 11 planes back-to-back at its address, then darkness for 15/16ths of the frame). Swapping the scan order to plane-major — all 16 rows at plane 0, then all 16 at plane 1, … — costs **zero time** (same slots, same total) but spreads each row's light into 11 bursts across the frame. It's the subfield-distribution trick plasma TVs and commercial LED controllers use, and in our engine it's literally swapping which of two counters increments fast. Implementing both:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-07-30T20:42:44.644Z*

**The new engine is live — go look at `will-voidrainbow` right now.** This is the biggest single perceptual jump we've made: **176.9 Hz refresh** (1.5× — now within shouting distance of v1's 430 Hz on a wall with 3× the pixels) *and* plane-distributed scanning, so each row lights **11 times per frame** instead of one burst per frame. Same brightness, same colors, same everything else.

Before I flash it permanent, tell me two things:

1. **The streaking during fast motion** — how much did it recede? Between the refresh jump and the subfield distribution, this should be the dominant improvement; if trace remains, the next rung (splitting the MSB plane into two half-slots at opposite frame phases, ~4% time cost for another ~2× perceptual refresh) is ready to build.
2. **Any color-bleed-toward-white on the far panels?** That's the 30 MHz shift-clock SI signature — these HATs ran 37.5 MHz on v1's 4-panel chains so I rate it unlikely at 6, but your eyes + the D9–D11 LEDs (should stay dark) are the verdict. If it shows, I drop the scan PLL a notch (50 or 45 MHz — still a big refresh win) while you decide about series termination.

On your brightness observation making sense now: the SW5 knob trades OE time against row time, so dimmer settings literally scan faster — that's why all-off (default) wasn't the minimum-tearing setting by accident; with the 60 MHz clock every setting moved up proportionally (unit=1 now scans at ~206 Hz, unit=15 at ~108 Hz).

And your instinct that "something seems off" versus the RP2350 — the honest answer is it was never *off*, it was *slower*: the RP2350 v1 drove 2 chains × 4 panels at 37.5 MHz shift = ~430 Hz refresh; the FPGA drives 4 chains × 6 panels, which is 6× the pixel throughput per sweep, and we'd been running the shift clock conservatively since the unbuffered bring-up days. Today's build closes most of that gap, and if it proves SI-clean we still have headroom (the engine times at 89 MHz; the panels and HATs, per v1, tolerate 37.5 MHz shift = 75 MHz scan) to push refresh past 220 Hz later. Verdict, please — streaks and bleed.

*2026-08-01T04:17:02.300Z*

Okay after a day soldering at the bench and experimenting with different jumper lengths, twistings, series resistors - I have all four chains rendering cleanly from end to end. Initially I had noise starting then cascading after about 3 panels on two chanels and 5 panels on two chains. After experimenting: it turns out that matching jumper length exactly was the answer. I tried adding a 22ohm resistor to the CLK and OE signal very close to the ECP5 (for the jumpers between the ECP5 and the HATs) and that actually made the signal worse. Once I measured out the jumper lengths, including accounting for the "shortening" caused by twisting the CLK signal which I missed during the first attempt: the signal runs through flawlessly end to end. 

That said - still some streaking. 16 px tall distortion that I can see "flow" down the panel. Zero bleed. Color is exact with no noise/jitter/artifacts. Only extremely smooth horizontal displacement/seams every 16 pixels across the entire panel during fast horizontal motion.

If you have anything in mind to correct, please give it a shot. Meanwhile I also had a few questions while working through this and thinking about it. If you could give me some insight it will help me understand better:

- I may revist building the HAT, but will likely do that in a separate session so it can be focuses specifically on the HAT and not carry a lot of unnecessary context. That said - if I added two four additional HUB75 connections (so an additional 4x R1G1B1R2...) that would require 24 additional pins. We have been using J32 and J33, which are now essentially completely occupied. Where would the additional 24 signals connect? Keep in mind my goal would be to make the connection "tight" in the sense that I don't want to run jumpers, so my mind goes to HAT. I'm a little less sure about making a hat that mates with the ECP5 dev board across multiple spaced out jumpers. Just curious your thoughts here and am open to suggestions.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-08-01T04:20:50.916Z*

That jumper-length discovery is a genuinely satisfying piece of bench engineering — and your instinct about *why* is right: at 30 MHz over short runs, reflections weren't the enemy, **skew** was. Matching lengths matched propagation delays, so CLK and data arrive at every HAT with aligned setup/hold margins; the series resistor made things worse because it slowed the CLK edge into an already-marginal skew budget. (And catching the twist-shortening on the second pass — that's the detail that separates working from almost-working.)

Now the seams — because your description is now precise enough to name the mechanism exactly, and it points at a fix I can build today.

**What the seam is:** within each 16-row block, rows light in address order, 0→15, spread across the sweep. So during horizontal motion, row 15 shows the scene almost one full sweep *later* than row 0 — the image shears smoothly by (motion speed × sweep time) down each block, then **snaps back at the block boundary** where row 16 (address 0 again) is back at the start of the cycle. A sawtooth: smooth displacement, seam every 16 px, phase crawling downward. It's pure multiplexing physics — v1 had it too, but at 430 Hz the shear was ~2.4× smaller and sat below your visual threshold. At 177 Hz it peeks out.

**Quick experiment you can run right now, zero rebuild:** flip SW5 to value 4 (position 3 ON alone) — that's quarter... half brightness and **~272 Hz**; value 2 gives ~358 Hz; value 1 gives **~409 Hz ≈ v1's refresh** at low brightness. If the seams melt away as you climb, we've confirmed the threshold — that's the calibration data for everything below.

**The real fix at full brightness — MSB subfield splitting.** Here's the insight: 75% of every pixel's light lives in planes 9 and 10. Currently each row emits that light **once** per sweep — one big burst, which is what your eye tracks into a sawtooth. But splitting plane 10 into **four** quarter-length slots and plane 9 into **two** halves, spread evenly across the sweep, makes each row's dominant light arrive ~4× per sweep — the effective motion-sampling rate of the bright content approaches **700 Hz** — and because those slots are display-bound, the cost is just their latch overhead: **~0.7% of refresh** (176.9 → 175.5 Hz). Same brightness, same colors, seam amplitude divided by roughly the split factor. This is precisely the trick commercial LED controllers and plasma TVs used. Building it:

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-08-01T04:25:41.667Z*

**The subfield-split engine is live on the wall now** (SRAM — I'll burn it to flash once you confirm; a power cycle would revert to the previous build until then). 22/22 sims green, including proof that the split changes no pixel's total light. Watch the same fast-motion content: the seams should drop to roughly a quarter of their amplitude. If you want to push further, the SW5 knob now stacks with it — value 4 ≈ 270 Hz at half brightness would put the *scan* rate itself near v1 territory; that's also useful calibration data for whether we ever want the 75 MHz PLL step.

**What just shipped, conceptually:** the seam is address-sweep shear — row 15 of each block lights almost a full sweep later than row 0, and the accumulated displacement snaps back at every block boundary. You can't eliminate it (it's what 1/16 multiplexing *is*), but you can shrink how much the world moves per light-burst. The engine now walks a compile-time *schedule* instead of plane order: plane 10 appears as **four** quarter-length slots and plane 9 as **two** halves, interleaved with the small planes across the sweep. Since those two planes carry 75% of all emitted light, each row's dominant light now lands ~4× per sweep — effective motion sampling around 700 Hz — for 0.7% of refresh (175.5 Hz) and zero brightness cost.

## Your questions

**Where would 4 more chains' 24 signals go?** The Versa expansion headers — **J39 (~19 usable GPIOs) and J40 (~30)**, both 2×20s like J32/J33. The budget was actually pre-planned in `WING-BOARD.md`: 8 chains = 48 data + 7 shared control = 55 pins, and J32+J33+J40 alone covers it (62). On the "tight, no jumpers" goal — your skepticism about a multi-header mezzanine is half right. It *is* doable: the EVN is a fixed board with published mechanicals, all four headers are through-hole on the top face, so a single PCB with receptacles at measured positions (calipers + the UG drawing, mounting holes as datum) is a classic mezzanine — but you get one shot at sub-millimeter placement across ~15 cm of board, and a respin if it's off. The pragmatic alternative your SI adventure actually argues for: a single "backplane" HAT that receives **short keyed IDC ribbons** from each header. Not loose jumpers — polarized connectors, and here's the kicker: ribbon conductors are *inherently length-matched*, which is precisely the property you spent today creating by hand. My recommendation for the future HAT session: IDC-backplane unless you specifically want the mezzanine as a KiCad precision exercise.

**The Colorlight 5A-75B** is, delightfully, the commercial embodiment of this exact project: an ECP5 (the smaller 25F) + 8 level-shifted HUB75 ports + SDRAM, doing BCM scan-out — a "receiver card" from the LED-wall industry. Stock operation: a "sender" (PC software or a sender box) streams raw pixels over **Ethernet** as low-level layer-2 frames; port one is the input, port two is a *daisy-chain output* so receivers chain card-to-card across giant walls. So the ethernet ports replace our DPI ingest, and its FPGA does what our translator does. Two notes for your drawer-inventory: it's fully reverse-engineered (the `chubby75` project) so people flash *custom* gateware — our translator could in principle port to it, except the 25F has only 1.0 Mbit of block RAM, so the framebuffer would have to live in its SDRAM (a new memory-controller project). And to set expectations: it would *not* dodge the seams — they're multiplex physics, and commercial cards fight them with exactly the tricks we're now implementing (high refresh + subfield distribution). Its genuine future value: an Ethernet-fed wall with no Pi mated to the FPGA, if that architecture ever tempts you.

Tell me how the seams look with the splits live — if it's where you want it, I'll flash it permanent; if it's close-but-not-quite, the next rungs are ready (plane 10 ×8 splits, or the 75 MHz scan PLL riding on your now-pristine wiring).

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-08-01T05:25:06.715Z*

This is an excellent piece of detective work — the power-cycle-to-flash revert explains *everything* about your confusing week, including your bench results. Let me answer the questions first, because your SW5 confusion is actually the **confirming diagnosis**, then give you two experiments to run before you commit to building boards.

**Why SW5 has zero effect on the corruption:** the brightness knob changes `unit` — the OE display durations — which changes *refresh rate*. But it never touches the **shift clock**: the rate data marches through the panels is the PLL output ÷ 2, compiled into the bitstream (currently 30 MHz). The corruption happens *during shifting* — it's the data/clock timing at 30 MHz that's failing — so every brightness setting corrupts identically while the seams (a refresh phenomenon) responded to the knob. Your observation cleanly separates the two artifact classes: **seams = refresh physics (fixed by the fast builds), corruption = shift-clock SI (the cliff between 20 and 30 MHz)**. And yes — the flash build is exactly 2/3 the clock: 20 MHz shift vs 30. The cliff is somewhere in between.

**Why the ribbon swap did nothing:** each panel's input '245s *regenerate* the signals panel-to-panel, so the HAT→panel ribbon isn't the accumulating segment. The 45°-skew-that-worsens-per-panel signature is accumulated clock-vs-data sampling error through those regeneration hops — and the margin that determines it is set at the *head end*: the ECP5→jumper→HAT leg. Which is also the one segment v1 never had — the RP2350 plugged *directly into* the HAT socket, zero wire, which is how v1 ran 37.5 MHz clean. Our jumper leg is the new tax.

**Before any new boards, two cheap experiments.** Your "22 Ω made it worse" result is a real clue: slowing the clock edge hurt — suggesting the edges arriving at the '245s are already too lazy. And indeed I've been driving the wall pins with `SLEWRATE="SLOW"` — a leftover from the unbuffered direct-drive bench days that's plausibly costing several ns of timing margin at 30 MHz. So: **build A = 60 MHz with FAST slew** (tests whether crisp edges rescue 30 MHz shift), **build B = 50 MHz** (25 MHz shift — bisects the cliff; still 146 Hz, which with subfield splitting may render the seams invisible anyway). Building both:

**Build A (60 MHz scan, FAST slew) is on the wall right now** — this is the one-variable test of the lazy-edge hypothesis. Look at the corruption pattern: if the cascade is gone (or dramatically reduced), the SLOW slew rate was eating our timing margin and we keep 175 Hz at full brightness.

If A still corrupts, the fallback is one command away — both bitstreams are staged on the Pi:

```fish
# Build B: 48 MHz scan / 24 MHz shift (bisects the cliff), 140.4 Hz refresh:
ssh rpi5 "openFPGALoader -b ecp5_evn /tmp/top_slow50.bit"
# Back to build A (60 MHz + FAST):
ssh rpi5 "openFPGALoader -b ecp5_evn /tmp/top_fast60.bit"
```

(Both SRAM-only — the flash still holds the known-good 20 MHz build as the safety net, so a power cycle always lands somewhere clean.)

How to read the outcomes:

- **A clean** → best case: edges were the whole problem; we flash A, keep 175.5 Hz + subfield splits, and no new boards are needed. There may even be headroom left for the 75 MHz someday.
- **A corrupts, B clean** → the cliff is between 24 and 30 MHz: we flash B at 140 Hz — which with the subfield splitting (dominant light ~4×/sweep ≈ 560 Hz effective) should still keep the seams invisible; judge on content. Board-building becomes optional polish.
- **Both corrupt** → the budget is tighter than the bisect, we settle at a verified point (I build 44/42 MHz variants in minutes), and *then* the new-boards conversation is real — where the right build isn't two more HATs but the terminated wing/backplane design, which is exactly the separate focused session you proposed.

One more note on your bench day so it doesn't feel wasted: the length-matching work wasn't invalidated — it's necessary-but-not-sufficient. At whatever clock we settle, matched skew is what keeps the margin you have; it just couldn't create margin that the 30 MHz + slow-edge combination had already spent. Tell me what A shows.

## 🤖 Assistant  
*2026-08-01T05:38:48.289Z*

**Flashed, self-booting, documented. The wall is done.**

The final state, for the record: 48 MHz scan → 24 MHz shift, 11 BCM planes with MSB subfield splitting, overlap engine, **140.4 Hz refresh with ~560 Hz effective motion sampling** at full brightness — clean end to end, no streaking, hardware brightness knob on SW5, boots itself from flash, reflashes over SSH. And the engineering knowledge that got here is now written down, not tribal: the SI cliff is *measured* (24 clean / 30 fails / slew irrelevant — so it's the head-end timing budget, which means your matched jumpers are load-bearing and any future speed push is a board-design problem, not a settings problem), and the seam mechanism plus its mitigations are in `SCANOUT.md` for whenever you read back through.

Worth pausing on what this project actually was, since you framed it as learning from day one. Twenty-five days ago the toolchain wasn't installed. Since then you've: brought up an FPGA from blinky through PLLs, UARTs, and clock-domain crossings; built a golden-model simulation culture that caught bugs before hardware ever saw them; shipped a video-capture pipeline, a double-buffered CDC, and a BCM scan-out engine with subfield scheduling — the same tricks commercial LED controllers use; debugged real signal integrity with real instruments and won; and ended with a self-contained appliance running your own shaders on 24 panels. That's not "tried an FPGA" — that's the whole discipline, end to end, with the scars to prove which parts matter.

What remains on the roadmap is entirely optional dessert, whenever the mood strikes:

- **120 Hz DPI** — the last motion-quality lever (25 MHz pixel clock; the capture margins and D9–D11 canaries are ready for it).
- **The backplane/mezzanine HAT session** — only if you ever want >24 MHz shift or the 8-chain layout; you now have the measured requirements to design against.
- **X2 200 MHz standalone clock** — only matters if the Pi's USB ever goes away.
- **Phase 5: the audio-DSP experiment** — mic → FFT in fabric → the FPGA as a feed-v3 sender. The canonical "second FPGA project," and all its prerequisite skills are ones you now have.

Enjoy the wall, Will. It's been a genuinely great build.
